Hidden variable theory for non-relativistic QED: the critical role of selection rules
Hiroshi Ishikawa

Abstract
Quantum theory, despite its remarkable success, struggles to represent certain experimental data, particularly those involving integer functions and deterministic relations between quantum jumps. We address this limitation by proposing a hidden variable theory compatible with non-relativistic quantum electrodynamics (QED). Our approach introduces logical variables to describe propositions about the occupation of stationary states, based on three key assumptions: (i) the probabilities of propositions are predictable by quantum theory, (ii) the truth values of propositions conform to Boolean logic, and (iii) propositions satisfy a novel selection rule: Tr [ K ^ ( t ) ρ ^ K ^ † ( t ) ] = Tr [ ( 𝒫 K ^ ( t ) ) ρ ^ ( 𝒫 K ^ {\rm{Tr}}[\hat{K}(t)\hat{\rho}\hat{K}^{\dagger}(t)]\,{=}\,{\rm{Tr}}[({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}(t))\hat{\rho}({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K} ( t ) ) † ] (t))^{\dagger}] . Here, K ^ ​ ( t ) \hat{K}(t) is the product of projection operators for the given propositions, and 𝒫 ​ K ^ ​ ( t ) {\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}(t) denotes K ^ ​ ( t ) \hat{K}(t) rearranged arbitrarily. This selection rule extends the consistency condition in the Consistent Histories approach, relaxing constraints imposed by no-go theorems on logical variable representation, joint probability existence, and measurement contextuality. Consequently, our theory successfully describes the essential properties of individual trials, including the discreteness of quantum jumps, the continuity of classical trajectories, and deterministic relations between entangled subsystems. It achieves this without conflicting with quantum equations of motion, canonical commutation relations, or the operator ordering in quantum coherence functions. The direct description of individual trials made possible by this approach sheds new light on the discrete nature of quantum phenomena and the essence of reality.



1
Introduction

The difficulties quantum theory encounters in describing integer functions are widely recognized.
This issue, however, has often been understood in the context of paradoxes, making a clear initial problem statement critical for avoiding logical pitfalls.
To better understand the paradoxical nature of this problem, it is instructive to revisit Einstein’s seminal question: “Can quantum-mechanical description of physical reality be considered complete?”
[
1
,
2
]
.
Today, this inquiry requires reframing, as subsequent research
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
has refuted Einstein’s assumption of local point particles as the basis for modeling physical reality.
Revisiting the experimental observations that motivated Einstein’s question
[
7
]
reveals his primary concern: the discontinuous change in energy inferred from its dependence on the interger function, the number of photoemitted electrons during light-matter interactions.
This realization leads us to a more pertinent question for our times: “Can the quantum-mechanical description of
integer functions
be considered complete?”

Contrary to what one might expect, the answer to this revised question is an unequivocal no.
This definitive conclusion is rooted in the fundamental restrictions inherent to the standard formulation of quantum theory, as evidenced by various no-go theorems concerning the representation of integer functions and individual trials
[
8
,
9
]
.
In this respect, Einstein’s critique about the incompleteness of quantum theory was essentially correct, albeit based on inappropriate assumptions about physical reality.
We now recognize that the core issue lies in the critical gap between experimental observations and theoretical descriptions of integer functions.
This limitation underscores the urgent need for a novel approach that transcends the constraints imposed by existing no-go theorems.

To address this fundamental problem, we clearly restate the experimental data to be explaned and modify the selection rules for logical propositions in quantum theory, which have been either implicitly assumed or treated as auxiliary conditions.
Specifically, we introduce an alternative to the orthogonality condition in von Neumann-Lüders’ theory
[
10
]
or the consistency condition in the Consistent Histories approach
[
11
]
.
This allows us to construct a novel hidden variable theory that can represent integer functions without contradicting the laws of quantum theory.
This seemingly minor adjustment yields profound consequences: it harmonizes quantum theory with Boolean logic, enables the construction of a stationary-state-based model of reality, and allows for the definition of instantaneous values of physical quantities in individual trials.
This paper thus elucidates the physics brought about by this new selection rule.

The paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
establishes the physical background for extending the theory beyond the standard formulation of quantum theory.
We identify experimental data unexplained by the standard formulation, outline limitations of existing theories, and briefly summarize key features of our approach.
Section
3
derives a new selection rule to harmonize Boolean logic with quantum theory, enabling the definition of logical variables whose expectation values are predictable by quantum theory.
Section
4
presents our model of reality and delineates the assumptions necessary for constructing the hidden variable theory.
Section
5
summarizes the key features of the resulting theory, including: (i) derivation of deterministic relations in entangled subsystems, (ii) definition of instantaneous values of physical quantities, (iii) explanation of measurement contextuality through the new selection rule, (iv) formulation of a classical approximation for individual trials, (v) revised explanation of the Bell and CHSH inequalities, and (vi) advantage over the light quantum hypothesis.
Section
6
addresses unresolved aspects of our theory, while Section
7
provides overall conclusions.
Appendices offer in-depth explanations of specific topics, and supplementary materials present calculation examples and various deterministic relations in well-known quantum paradoxes.

This paper focuses on the phenomenological representation of integer functions observed in quantum optics experiments.
Therefore, we minimize references to literature not directly related to the representation of integer functions.
In particular, we believe that connections to quantum information theory
[
12
]
or the interpretations of quantum theory
[
13
]
require separate discussion.
We also omit the discussion on relativistic theory, instead highlighting unanswered questions about the definition of stationary states in non-relativistic theories.
As an initial attempt to construct a physical theory, we prioritize conceptual development over mathematical rigor.
This approach should be justified at this stage, given that Hilbert space construction is common to conventional theories
[
9
,
11
,
14
]
.
On the other hand, we have confirmed that our theory
[
15
]
reproduces standard formulas
[
16
,
17
]
for light detection probability, quantum coherence functions, and counting statistics.
While most calculations are straightforward, some care is needed to describe double-slit experiments since it requires a realistic model of measuring instruments (an array of photodetectors, each with multiple absorption centers).

2
Preliminary considerations and methodology summary

2.1
Experimental data unexplained by standard quantum theory

Figure 1:
Schematic representation of two types of experimental data in a photon counting experiment.
(a) An integer-valued function obtained from an individual trial, as described by Eq.(
1
). The trial number is denoted by
j
, and individual events are characterized by discontinuities in these integer functions.
(b) A real-valued function defined as the expectation value of the integer function, as given by Eq.(
2
). For ensembles where individual events occur at random times, the time evolution of the expectation value appears continuous. This continuity arises from the averaging of discontinuous changes in the individual events through the limit operation
\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{N}({1}/{N})
.

The standard formulation of quantum theory encounters a significant challenge when attempting to represent the number of quantum jumps observed in photon counting experiments
[
18
,
19
]
and quantum jump experiments
[
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
.
A distinctive feature of these experimental data lies in their unique mathematical structure.
Specifically, these experiments typically yield data on the occurrence times of quantum jumps, denoted by
t^{(j)}_{i}
.
The number of quantum jumps in the
j
-th trial is defined by substituting
t^{(j)}_{i}
into the following equation:

\displaystyle n^{(j)}(t)\equiv\sum_{i}\Theta(t-t^{(j)}_{i}),\quad\Theta(t)\equiv\begin{cases}0,&t<0\\
1,&t\geqq 0\end{cases}

(1)

(Fig.
1
(a)).
Here,
j\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{N}}
is the trial number,
i\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{N}}
is the sequential number of quantum jumps within a trial (
t^{(j)}_{i}\,{<}\,t^{(j)}_{i+1}
), and
\Theta(t)
is an integer-valued step function.
The expectation value of this integer function is defined as

\displaystyle\langle n(t)\rangle\equiv\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}n^{(j)}(t).

(2)

This expectation value typically manifests as a continuous real-valued function due to the stochastic variation of
t^{(j)}_{i}
(Fig.
1
(b)).
For this continuous experimental data, the equation of motion, combined with the Born rule, establishes the following relationship:

\displaystyle\langle n(t)\rangle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\hat{n}\hat{\rho}(t)\Big],\quad\frac{d}{dt}\hat{\rho}(t)={\mathcal{L}}\hat{\rho}(t),

(3)

where
\hat{\rho}(t)
is the density operator for the given ensemble,
{\mathcal{L}}
is the Liouville super-operator specifying the von Neumann equation
[
9
]
, and
\hat{n}
is an experiment-specific operator
[
15
]
.

The problem becomes evident when we introduce the assumption that the integer function
n^{(j)}(t)
can be represented by a density operator
\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)
as

\displaystyle n^{(j)}(t)\overset{?}{=}{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\hat{n}\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)\Big],\quad\forall j.

(4)

Firstly, individual integer functions are non-differentiable at quantum jump occurrences,
t\,{=}\,t^{(j)}_{i}
.
Therefore, it is unreasonable to assume that
\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)
satisfies an equation of motion of the same form as Eq.(
3
):

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)\overset{?}{=}{\mathcal{L}}\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t).

(5)

Secondly, even without assuming the differentiability of
\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)
, a problem arises when
n^{(j)}(t)
is a logical variable describing the occupation of orthonormalized quantum states
\{|\phi_{1}\rangle,\ldots,|\phi_{M}\rangle\}
:

\displaystyle n^{(j)}_{\mu}(t)\equiv\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{when $|\phi^{(j)}(t)\rangle$ is $|\phi_{\mu}\rangle$}\\
0,&\mbox{when $|\phi^{(j)}(t)\rangle$ is $|\phi_{\mu}\rangle$}\end{cases},\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,\mu\,{\leqq}\,M.

(6)

Appendix
A
demonstrates that the combination of Eq.(
4
) and Eq.(
6
) leads to a conclusion contradicting the superposition principle.
The standard interpretation
[
8
, §7.2]
of Gleason’s theorem
[
25
]
also supports the impossibility of assuming the combination of Eq.(
4
) and Eq.(
6
).
This paradox reveals a fundamental limitation of the standard formulation of quantum theory: while it can predict the expectation values of integer functions, it fails to represent individual integer functions themselves.
In other words, quantum theory—operating under the premise of describing all experimental data via density operators—cannot represent
integer functions whose expectation values are predictable by quantum theory
.

2.2
Problems with conventional theories

To address these fundamental issues with sufficient specificity, we focus our analysis on experimental observations in quantum optics
[
26
]
.
In particular, we consider how to describe the aforementioned integer functions within the framework of non-relativistic QED.
We find that the discontinuous behavior of these integer functions has traditionally been explained using the concept of photons
[
7
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
]
.
However, modern quantum optics theory
[
16
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
has largely rendered the photon concept unnecessary for most applications.
As Lamb described in his “anti-photon” paper
[
37
]
, this shift results from two key developments: (i) the quantization of both atomic systems and radiation fields by non-relativistic QED
[
38
,
39
,
40
]
and (ii) the introduction of modern wave representations through the quantum theory of coherence
[
41
]
and the semi-classical theory of radiation
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
.
Given this shift away from the photon concept, it seems reasonable to explain the discontinuous behavior of integer functions based solely on quantum jumps between stationary states
[
38
,
45
]
.
Contrary to expectations, however, this approach does not work in the standard formulation.
This is because if all experimental facts must be represented within Hilbert space, then quantum jumps cannot be a discontinuous phenomenon
[
46
]
(§
1
).
From this perspective, the conceptual confusion surrounding photons and quantum jumps
[
46
,
47
,
48
]
is intrinsically linked to the lack of a formalism capable of representing integer functions.

To address these conceptual challenges and identify the core issues in conventional theory, it is beneficial to introduce phenomenological concepts.
First, if we define
individual events
as discontinuous changes in integer functions (Fig.
1
(a)), the discontinuous changes can be described without assuming the existence of photons and quantum jumps.
We can also see that individual events are non-differentiable by definition, meaning that they cannot be derived from the quantum equations of motion, especially under ordinary mathematics where no derivative exists for integer functions (§
2.1
).
Second, to highlight the existence of two types of experimental data for a given time
t
(Fig.
1
), it is useful to refer to the integer functions observed in individual trials (Fig.
1
(a)) as the
world of reality
, while the real-valued function defined in a statistical limit (Fig.
1
(b)) as the
world of expectation
.
This distinction reveals a fundamental dichotomy: the quantum equation of motion holds in the world of expectation but fails in the world of reality.
Note that individual events occur only in the world of reality.
We then find that quantum theory is a theory of the world of expectation (i.e., real-valued function), requiring
a hidden variable theory to describe the world of reality (i.e., individual integer functions)
.
Crucially, to break away from the contradictions surrounding photons and local reality, a hidden variable theory in this sense must focus on stationary states rather than local point particles such as photons.

According to the above discussion, special attention must be paid to the no-go theorem for theories that describe the stationary state occupation in terms of logical variables.
From our point of view, this type of no-go theorem makes sense when one assumes a combination of Bohr’s interpretation of quantum theory (rejecting hidden variables)
[
45
,
49
,
50
,
51
,
52
]
with Einstein’s model of physical reality (local point particles)
[
1
,
2
]
.
Under this combination, any theory that represents logical variables using Hilbert space concepts (density operators or projection operators) is prohibited by Gleason’s theorem (§
2.1
).
Additionally, hidden variable theories based on the locality assumption are rejected by Bell’s theorem and related experiments
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
, while theories assigning integer values to individual trials face contradictions from the CHSH theorem
[
5
]
and the Kochen-Specker theorem
[
8
,
53
]
.
In contrast, a hidden variable theory aligned with the stationary state model would likely combine Einstein’s interpretation of quantum theory (allowing hidden variables)
[
2
,
54
]
with Bohr’s model of physical reality (assuming stationary states)
[
38
,
45
]
.
This inverse combination is independent of Bell’s theorem’s locality assumption, resolving Gleason’s theorem’s constraints by assuming only Eq.(
6
) (See Appendix
A
).
In this respect, the essential challenge for hidden variable theories lies in avoiding contradictions related to measurement contextuality, as exemplified by the CHSH and Kochen-Specker theorems
[
8
]
.

Meanwhile, quantum jump theories not assuming hidden variables were extensively studied after quantum jumps were experimentally observed in the 1980s.
Among these theories, Quantum Jump Approach in Quantum Optics
[
55
,
56
]
focused on single quantum systems described by master equations, developing numerical methods that explicitly incorporate the state vector collapse due to decoherence
[
57
]
.
Furthermore, Consistent Histories Approach to Quantum Theory
[
11
,
58
]
focused on the chains of quantum jumps (a.k.a. “Histories”), demonstrating how to calculate the probability that an individual History is realized.
Among these developments, the Consistent Histories approach made a significant contribution by showing that it is possible to construct a theory compatible with both quantum theory and Boolean logic, by limiting the range of Histories using selection rules (consistency conditions)
[
11
]
.
Nevertheless, these theories did not acknowledge the existence of hidden variables, assigning continuous values (0 to 1) to stationary state populations
[
11
,
56
]
.
This is why conventional quantum jump theories cannot represent integer functions—the fundamental limitation of theories describing all experimental data using Hilbert space concepts.
Although significant progress has been made in the theory of open quantum systems
[
9
,
59
]
and quantum information theory
[
13
,
60
]
, this limitation still seems to persist in more recent theories.

2.3
Key features of our approach

To overcome this limitation of the standard formulation, we propose a novel theoretical framework that integrates (i) Einstein’s interpretation of quantum theory, (ii) Bohr’s model of physical reality, and (iii) the methodology of the Consistent Histories approach.
In other words, we consider a hidden variable theory that employs Boolean logical variables to describe stationary state occupation, introducing a novel selection rule to reconcile Boolean logic and quantum theory.
More specifically, our theory centers on
propositions whose probabilities are predictable by quantum theory
, constituting a dual framework such that their truth values are represented by Boolean logical variables and their probabilities are calculated using the density operator (Fig.
2
).
A key feature of this theoretical framework is the selection rule for determining allowable propositions, the form of which is determined based on two fundamental requirements: (i) the uniqueness of the probability of the result of a Boolean operation and (ii) the consistency of the time ordering of the operators involved.
This selection rule, which we refer to below as the compatibility condition, serves as a necessary condition for consistently defining the conjunction and disjunction of propositions.
Remarkably, it allows for a wider range of propositions than conventional selection rules, such as the orthogonality condition of von Neumann-Lüders’ theory and the consistency condition of the Consistent Histories approach.

of logical variables

Stationary

states

Boolean logic

Exclusivity

World of reality

(Integer function)

Expectation value

of logical variables

Density

operator

Born rule

Equation of motion

World of expectation

(Real-valued function)

expectation values

Compatibility

condition

Figure 2:
The overall structure of the theory
. A two-layer theory is considered to capture the mathematical structure of propositions whose probabilities are predictable by quantum theory. Upper frame: The truth values of propositions are represented by the instantaneous values of logical variables, which describe the stationary state occupations. The truth values of propositions are assumed to follow the laws of Boolean logic and exclusivity. Lower frame: The probabilities of propositions are represented by the expectation values of logical variables, which are determined by the density operator. The probabilities of propositions are assumed to obey the laws of quantum theory (i.e., the Born rule and the equations of motion). Left line: The truth values and probabilities of propositions are linked through the definition of expectation values of logical variables. Right line: The contradiction between the laws of Boolean logic and quantum theory is avoided by a new selection rule (i.e., compatibility condition).

The modification of the selection rule has the following effects:

1.

The existence of joint probability is guaranteed for propositions that are true only for a particular ensemble and at a given instant of time.
This means that even for propositions with non-commutative operators, joint probability can be defined by reducing the precision of proposition representation.

2.

If logical variables are used to define instantaneous values of physical quantities, arithmetic operations are prohibited between physical quantities that involve propositions not satisfying the compatibility condition.
This overcomes the inconsistencies regarding measurement contextuality (e.g., the CHSH and Kochen-Specker theorems).

3.

Canonical variables and canonical momenta meet the compatibility condition when the precision of representation is adjusted under classical approximation.
This eliminates the constraint on joint probabilities by Nelson’s theorem
[
9
]
, allowing us to derive the complementarity principle and classical equations of motion.

4.

The resolution of the known contradictions in major no-go theorems justifies the use of logical variables (hidden variables) to represent propositions whose probabilities are predictable by quantum theory.
This also makes it possible to describe integer functions whose expectation values are predictable by quantum theory.

5.

Deterministic relations between individual events can be explicitly expressed as equations between logical variables.
In fundamental problems such as the photoionization of hydrogen atoms and spin measurements in a spin-singlet system, deterministic relations in entangled subsystems can be derived from the first principles.

6.

For propositions whose joint probabilities can be calculated by quantum theory, the same joint probability formulas as in quantum theory are obtained.
The definition of conjunction ensures the time and normal ordering of operators, leading to the standard formulas for quantum coherence functions in quantum optics
[
16
,
17
]
.

7.

For propositions whose joint probabilities cannot be calculated by quantum theory, this theory can represent deterministic relations that cannot be represented by the standard formulation.
Relatedly, non-deterministic hidden variables may exhibit deterministic behavior when the precision of proposition representation is reduced.

Among these key features, the ability to express deterministic relations in individual trials (#7) is not found in previous theories, so its basic properties will be briefly described below.
First of all, the most fundamental hidden variable in our theory is the logical variable that describes the occupation of the stationary state
|\phi\rangle
of the entire system:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{Proposition `${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$' is True},\\
0,&\mbox{Proposition `${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$' is False}.\end{cases}

(7)

Here, the symbol ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t
’ represents a proposition ‘A stationary state
|\phi\rangle
is occupied at time
t
’.
Denoting the stationary state occupied at time
t
in the
j
-th trial as
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
, stationary state
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
will vary stochastically according to the laws of quantum theory.
Consequently, there is no special advantage in considering this type of hidden variable.
On the other hand, the stationary states of many-body systems are usually too complex to calculate, suggesting that a proposition with reduced precision is necessary to express experimental results.
Now, let
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}
denote the subspace of Hilbert space always containing
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
and let ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ denotes a proposition ‘The stationary states occupied at time
t
is contained in subspace
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}
’.
Then, proposition ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ is always true by definition, meaning that a logical variable

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{Proposition `${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}}),t$' is True}\\
0,&\mbox{Proposition `${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}}),t$' is False}\end{cases}

(8)

will always have the constant value 1.
It is this type of propositions that become describable by introducing the compatibility condition.
Significantly, the new hidden variable in Eq.(
8
) is deterministic while the hidden variable in Eq.(
7
) is non-deterministic.
Furthermore, when the subspace
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}
of Hilbert space can be decomposed into a direct product
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,1}}\}\!\!\}\,{\otimes}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,2}}\}\!\!\}
, the deterministic relation

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,1}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,2}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)=1.

(9)

is established between the propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,1}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,2}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’.
In this sense, the compatibility condition is a selection rule to describe deterministic relations in the world of reality, which sometimes allows us to derive deterministic laws for individual trials from the probabilistic laws of quantum theory.
Importantly, what matters in the laws of reality is no longer the time variation of logical variables, but conditions
{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,1}
and
{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,2}
that specify propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,1}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega,2}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ that always become true.

It should also be pointed out that the standard formulation sometimes fails to represent this type of deterministic relation.
To see this, suppose that the logical variables for propositions ‘
\mathrm{A}_{1},\,t_{1}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{A}_{2},\,t_{2}
’, each describing subsystems 1 and 2 and not necessarily equal 1, can be consistently defined as

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{l},t_{l}$'}\big)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{l}$' is True at time $t_{l}$}\\
0&\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{l}$' is False at time $t_{l}$}\end{cases},\quad l\in\{1,2\}.

(10)

We will later show that this is possible if the interaction between the subsystems is negligible at the time of interest.
When the subsystems are entangled, we can then derive a deterministic relation between propositions ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{1},t_{1}
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{2},t_{2}
’, namely

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{1},\,t_{1}$'})={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{2},\,t_{2}$'}),\quad\forall{j}.

(11)

Denoting the conjunction (AND) of ‘
\mathrm{A}_{1},\,t_{1}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{A}_{2},\,t_{2}
’ as ‘
\mathrm{A}_{1},\,t_{1}
’
\wedge
‘
\mathrm{A}_{2},\,t_{2}
’, this equation is equivalent to

\displaystyle\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{1},\,t_{1}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{A}_{2},\,t_{2}$'})\rangle=\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{1},\,t_{1}$'})\rangle=\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{2},\,t_{2}$'})\rangle,

(12)

which implies that the conditional probabilities are 1.
In previous theories, it was not possible to define the left-hand side of Eq.(
12
) when operators
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{1},\,t_{1}$'})
and
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{2},\,t_{2}$'})
do not commute (as per Nelson’s theorem).
Therefore, the only relation that can be safely expressed was

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\big\rangle=\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big\rangle.

(13)

We can now see that Eq.(
13
) can be derived from Eq.(
11
), but not vice versa.
For this reason, the equation linking two or more logical variables may express a law of reality that the standard formulation has failed to represent.
Specific examples of the laws of reality are Eq.(
7
) and Eq.(
8
), and more specific examples are the classical equations of motion and the light quantum hypothesis.
Interestingly, however, when the light quantum hypothesis is expressed in the form of Eq.(
11
), the corresponding operators do not have a consistent expression for different initial conditions.
This suggests that the stationary-state type model of reality, as postulated in our theory, is more reasonable than the light quantum hypothesis as a substructure of non-relativistic QED.

Brief summary of methodology

for exact formula.

​​​​​2

Stationary states

\hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}\,{=}\,{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}^{\dagger}\,\big(\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}\,{+}\,\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}\big){\hat{\mathsf{U}}},\penalty\ \hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}|\phi\rangle\,{=}\,E|\phi\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi\phi^{\prime}},\penalty\ \hat{1}\,{=}\,{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}^{\dagger}\,{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}\,{=}\,{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}\,{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}^{\dagger}

​​​​​3

Stationary state sets

\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}_{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}\,{\equiv}\,\{\,\forall|\phi\rangle\,|\penalty\ \hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}|\phi\rangle\,{=}\,E(\phi)|\phi\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}},\penalty\ \mbox{$|\phi\rangle$ satisfy C\,}\}

​​​​​4

Element propositions

‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}})
’
\equiv
‘One of the stationary states in
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}
is occupied at time
t
’

​​​​​5

Logical variables

{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`o($\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}$)'}\big)\,{\in}\,\{0,1\}

​​​​​6

Laws of Boolean logic

{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'})\,{=}\,{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'})\,{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'})
etc.

​​​​​7

Exclusivity

{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'})\,{=}\,0
for
\phi_{1}\,{\neq}\,\phi_{2}

​​​​​8

Complex propositions

‘A,
\{t\}
’
\,{\equiv}\,\bigwedge^{I}_{i=1}\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}$'}
, ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}
’:
i
-th elementary proposition

​​​​​9

Expectation values

\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\rangle\,{\equiv}\,{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'}}{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})}/{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'}}\,1}

​​​​​10

Born rule (1)

\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\rangle\,{=}\,{\mathrm{Tr}}[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0}$'})\,]
,
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\,{=}\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})

​​​​​11

Born rule (2)

{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}})$'})\,{=}\,\sum_{|\phi\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}}|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|

​​​​​12

Conversion rules

{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'})\,{=}\,\mathcal{T}::{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'})\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'})::
, etc.

​​​​​13

Equation of motion

{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'})\,{=}\,\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}})$'})\,\hat{U}(t)
,
\hat{U}(t)\,{\equiv}\,\exp({t}\hat{H}/{{\rm{i}}\hbar})

​​​​​14

Comatibility condition

{\rm{Tr}}[\hat{K}\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\hat{K}^{\dagger}]\,{=}\,{\rm{Tr}}[({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K})\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K})^{\dagger}]
,
\hat{K}(t)\,{\equiv}\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i_{1}},t$'})\,{\cdots}\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i_{\mathcal{I}}},t$'})

​​​​​0

Model of reality

{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'})\,{=}\,1,\penalty\ \Omega^{(j)}(t)\,{\in}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}

To summarize the discussion up to this point, we present in Table
1
a comparison of how various approaches to quantum theory represent the world of reality (i.e., individual integer functions) and the world of expectation (i.e., real-valued functions).
The standard formulation of quantum theory (#4) does not recognize the existence of hidden variables, excluding integer functions from theoretical descriptions
[
8
]
(Appendix
A
).
Similarly, recent quantum jump theories (#5 and #6) do not admit hidden variables, describing quantum jumps based on continuous changes in Hilbert space.
In contrast, our theory (#7) describes the world of expectation using density operators and the world of reality using Boolean logical variables.
Such a theory is only possible under a combination of (i) Einstein’s interpretation of quantum theory, (ii) Bohr’s model of physical reality, and (iii) a selection rule to harmonize Boolean logic and quantum theory.
A comprehensive list of necessary assumptions is shown in Table
2
.

2.5
Notation

In the following, a special notation is used to express propositions as concisely as possible.
First, propositions whose probabilities are predictable by quantum theory are distinguished by single quotation marks (‘
\cdots
’).
A time variable is added at the end if necessary.
For instance, the proposition ‘A is true at time
t
’ is denoted as ‘A,
t
’.
Second, when expressing propositions, a single stationary state
|\phi\rangle
is denoted as
{\{{\phi}\}}
by enclosing the quantum number in single braces.
Similarly, a set of stationary states satisfying a condition C is denoted as
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}
by enclosing the condition label in double curly braces.
To save symbols, the subspace of the Hilbert space spanned by the stationary states in set
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}
is also represented using the same symbol
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}
.
Third, the proposition describing stationary state occupation is denoted as ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\penalty\ })
’.
Specifically, ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t
’ represents a proposition ‘A stationary state
|\phi\rangle
is occupied at time
t
’, and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ represents a proposition ‘The stationary state occupied at time
t
is contained in subspace
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}
’.
Other symbols are defined according to quantum optics conventions whenever possible
[
16
,
33
]
.
The meanings of all symbols are summarized in Appendix
C
, but will be explained as necessary.

3
Definition of logical variables

3.1
Basic assumptions

In this section, we identify the combination of assumptions necessary to define Boolean logical variables whose expectation values are predictable by quantum theory.
To this end, we first make the following assumption:

Assumption A1
.

The truth value of a given proposition ‘A,
t
’ in individual trials can be expressed using the logical variable

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\equiv\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{`A' is True at time $t$}\\
0,&\mbox{`A' is False at time $t$}\end{cases},\quad j\,{\in}\,\mathbb{N},

(14)

where
j
is the trial number and
\mathbb{N}
denotes natural numbers (i.e., integers greater than 0).

Assumption A2
.

The expectation value of the logical variable can be uniquely determined as a real-valued function by

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\big\rangle\equiv\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\!\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}}{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\in{\mathbb{R}},

(15)

where ‘
\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}
’ is a proposition that becomes true for a given ensemble, and
\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}}
stands for the sum over the given ensemble satisfying ‘
\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}
’.
The sample number is defined by
N\,{\equiv}\,\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}}1
.

Assumption A3
.

The expectation value of the logical variable can be predicted using the Born rule and the formal solution of the equation of motion, namely

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\big\rangle

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big],

(16)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})

\displaystyle\equiv\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A'})\,\hat{U}(t),

(17)

where
\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})
is the density operator of the given ensemble and
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})
is an operator corresponding to ‘A,
t
’.
For clarity, we consider a closed system and assume that the time evolution operator
\hat{U}(t)\,{\equiv}\,\exp({t}\hat{H}/{{\rm{i}}\hbar})
is unitary and
\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})
is Hermitian.

Under these assumptions, the expectation value of a logical variable represents the probability of ‘A,
t
’ conditioned by ‘
\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}
’, as can be seen from

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\big\rangle\equiv\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}}{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})}{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}}1}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}\,\wedge\mbox{\scriptsize{`A,\,$t$'}}}1}{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}}1}.

(18)

This shows that Assumptions A1–A3 define a proposition ‘A,
t
’ whose probability is predictable by quantum theory.
Note that
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})
is always Hermitian and positive-semidefinite since
0\,{\leq}\,{\mathrm{Tr}}[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0}$'})\,]\,{\leq}\,1
by Eq.(
18
).
It follows that
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})
can be decomposed using the non-Hermitian operator
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})
into the following form:

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})={\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'}).

(19)

This means that Eqs.(
16
)–(
17
) can always be rewritten as follows
[
9
]
:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\big\rangle

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\,\Big],

(20)

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})

\displaystyle\equiv\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})\,\hat{U}(t).

(21)

Here, the operators
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})
and
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})
play a central role in the theory presented below, so we will refer to them as the
characteristic and partial characteristic operators
of ‘A,
t
’, respectively.
1
1

1

In our theory, the most general form of the partial characteristic operator
\hat{z}
is the product of the projection operators of propositions satisfying the compatibility condition, while in the Consistent Histories approach it is the product of the projection operators satisfying the consistency condition (i.e., the chain operator of History)
[
11
]
. The characteristic operator
\hat{x}
becomes significant only when considering general Boolean logic operations, as shown below.

Next, we assume that the logical variables further satisfy the laws of Boolean logic.

Assumption A4
.

Given a proposition ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and a proposition ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’, their negation (
\lnot
, NOT), conjunction (
\wedge
, AND), and disjunction (
\vee
, OR) are defined by

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{$\lnot$`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})

\displaystyle=1-{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}),\penalty\ \penalty\ {\forall}j,

(22)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),\penalty\ \penalty\ {\forall}j,

(23)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\vee$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})+{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})-{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),\penalty\ \penalty\ {\forall}j,\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(24)

where Eq.(
22
) holds for ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ as well as ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’.

Note that the combination of Assumptions A1–A4 is prohibited in the standard formulation.
In contrast, we introduce additional assumptions so that Assumptions A1 and A4 hold in the world of reality and Assumption A3 holds in the world of expectation.
Assuming that Assumption A3 also holds for the result of logical operations, we can immediately see that Assumptions A1–A4 are not closed by themselves.
More specifically, even if ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ satisfy Assumptions A1–A4, their conjunction and disjunction may not satisfy Assumption A3.
Taking the expectation value of Eqs.(
22
)–(
24
) and substituting Eq.(
16
), we see that Assumptions A1–A4 are consistent only if

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{$\lnot$`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\big[\,\big(1-{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big],

(25)

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big],

(26)

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\vee$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\big[\,\big({\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})+{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big)\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big].

(27)

Focusing on the case
t_{\mathrm{A}}\,{\neq}\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
, these equations are satisfied if the characteristic operators are defined as

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{$\lnot$`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})

\displaystyle=\hat{1}-{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}),

(28)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}::{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})::,

(29)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\vee$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle={\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})+{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})-{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),

(30)

where
\mathcal{T}
and
::\,::
are the time-ordering and normal-ordering symbols for partial characteristic operators, respectively.
It is easy to see that Eq.(
28
) and Eq.(
30
) are necessary for Eq.(
25
) and Eq.(
27
) to hold for any
\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})
.
On the other hand, Eq.(
29
) involves an operator product and must be derived from a heuristic consideration as described below.

3.2
Derivation of the compatibility condition

To derive Eq.(
29
), we return to the definitions of characteristic and partial characteristic operators and note that the following relationship exists:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big\rangle

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

(31)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\Big].

(32)

As the simplest case, let us consider propositions ‘A,
t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘B,
t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ that describe the occupation of single quantum states
|\mbox{A},t_{\mathrm{A}}\rangle
and
|\mbox{B},t_{\mathrm{B}}\rangle
, respectively.
In this case,
\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big\rangle
should physically correspond to the transition probability between
|\mbox{A},t_{\mathrm{A}}\rangle
and
|\mbox{B},t_{\mathrm{B}}\rangle

[
39
]
:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big\rangle=\big|\langle\mbox{A},t_{\mathrm{A}}|\mbox{B},t_{\mathrm{B}}\rangle\big|^{2}.

(33)

Meanwhile, by the Born rule, we have

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{=}\,|\mbox{A},t_{\mathrm{A}}\rangle\langle\mbox{A},t_{\mathrm{A}}|,\quad{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{=}\,|\mbox{B},t_{\mathrm{B}}\rangle\langle\mbox{B},t_{\mathrm{B}}|.

(34)

Comparing Eqs.(
32
)–(
34
), we find that
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})
must be the product of
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})
and
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

[
11
, Chap.10]
.
It then follows from the formal solution of the Heisenberg equation (Eq.(
17
)) that the partial characteristic operators must act on the density operator in ascending order of time as

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})=\begin{cases}{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),&t_{\mathrm{A}}>t_{\mathrm{B}},\\
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}),&t_{\mathrm{A}}<t_{\mathrm{B}}.\\
\end{cases}

(35)

Substituting Eq.(
35
) into Eq.(
19
), we obtain the case
t_{\mathrm{A}}\,{\neq}\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
of Eq.(
29
):

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle={\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

(36)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),&t_{\mathrm{A}}>t_{\mathrm{B}},\\
{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}),&t_{\mathrm{A}}<t_{\mathrm{B}}.\end{cases}

(37)

Even in previous theories, it has been believed that Eq.(
35
) holds in more general cases than assumed in Eq.(
34
).
For example, the Consistent Histories approach assumes that the chain operator of History can be defined when projection operators commute
[
11
, Chap.10]
.
Additionally, for general fundamental excitations, quantum correlation functions
[
16
, §11.12]
and many-body Green functions
[
61
, §3.7]
are defined as similar time- and normally-ordered products of creation and annihilation operators.
The validity of such generalizations should be judged by whether they can explain the known experimental facts.
Thus, to represent integer functions that previous theories cannot describe, we relax the existing constraints.
We assume that Eq.(
35
) holds as long as the partial characteristic operator of a single-time proposition is a projection operator.

Assumption A5
.

Boolean logic operations transform characteristic operators according to the following equations:

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{$\lnot$`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})

\displaystyle=\hat{1}-{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}),

(38)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}::{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})::

(39)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\vee$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),

\displaystyle={\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})+{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})-{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}).

(40)

Here,
\mathcal{T}
and
::\,::
are the time-ordering and normal-ordering symbols for partial characteristic operators, respectively.

Assumption A6
.

The partial characteristic operator of a single-time proposition is the projection operator onto a set of orthonormalized quantum states:

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})=\sum^{\mbox{\scriptsize`A'}}_{|a\rangle}|a\rangle\langle a|,\quad\langle a|a^{\prime}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}.

(41)

Here,
\sum^{\mbox{\scriptsize`A'}}_{|a\rangle}
denotes the sum over the quantum states such that proposition ‘A’ is true.
See Section
4
for a more specific definition of the set of quantum states.

We can now see that Eq.(
39
) (or Eq.(
37
)) takes different forms for
t_{\mathrm{A}}\!>\!t_{\mathrm{B}}
and
t_{\mathrm{A}}\!<\!t_{\mathrm{B}}
, indicating that additional assumptions are necessary to guarantee the consistency of Assumptions A1–A6.
Substituting Eq.(
37
) into Eq.(
31
), we find

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]&t_{\mathrm{A}}>t_{\mathrm{B}},\\
\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]&t_{\mathrm{A}}<t_{\mathrm{B}}.\end{cases}

(42)

For the probability of
\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}\,{\wedge}\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}
to be uniquely determined in the limit
t_{\mathrm{A}}\,{\to}\,t
and
t_{\mathrm{B}}\,{\to}\,t
, it is necessary to assume the following auxiliary condition:

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`B,\,$t$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B,\,$t$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`B,\,$t$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B,\,$t$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]

(43)

Accordingly, we make the following assumptions to harmonize the laws of quantum theory and the laws of Boolean logic at
t_{\mathrm{A}}\,{=}\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
.

Assumption A7
.

Any meaningful propositions ‘A’ and ‘B’ must satisfy the condition

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big],

(44)

where
\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\equiv}\,\hat{U}(t)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)
is the density operator in the Schrödinger picture.

Assumption A8
.

The time-ordering symbol
\mathcal{T}
and the normal-ordering symbol
::\,::
for the partial characteristic operator are defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}::{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}){\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})::\,

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),&t_{\mathrm{A}}>t_{\mathrm{B}},\\
{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}),&t_{\mathrm{A}}<t_{\mathrm{B}},\\
\Big({\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})&\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ +{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\Big)/2,&t_{\mathrm{A}}=t_{\mathrm{B}}.\end{cases}

(45)

For convenience of reference, we will refer to Eq.(
44
) in Assumption A7 as the
compatibility condition
between proposition ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and proposition ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’.
Note that it is necessary to assume the compatibility condition to justify Assumption A8.
Compatibility conditions for two or more propositions can be obtained in the same way (See §
B.2
).

3.3
Joint and conditional probabilities

Before introducing the model of reality, let us summarize what can be said based on the minimal assumptions described above.
Under Assumptions A1–A8, the joint probability of ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ can be defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}\,{\wedge}\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\equiv\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big\rangle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big].

(46)

The conditional probability of ‘A,
t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ under the information of ‘B,
t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ is given by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}|\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle}{\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle}=\frac{{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]}{{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]},

(47)

where we assumed
t_{\mathrm{A}}\,{>}\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
for simplicity.
Using Assumption A5 (Eq.(
39
)) and Assumption A8 (Eq.(
45
)), this conditioinal probability can be rewitten in the form

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}|\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{x}(\mbox{`A'},\,t_{\mathrm{A}})\,\hat{\rho}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}\big)\,\Big],\quad t_{\mathrm{A}}>t_{\mathrm{B}},

(48)

where the density operator conditioned by proposition ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}\left(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}\right)\equiv\frac{{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})}{{\rm{Tr}}\left[\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\,\right]},\quad t_{\mathrm{A}}>t_{\mathrm{B}}.

(49)

Marginal probabilities can also be defined consistently by the laws of Boolean logic.
On the other hand, the appearance of the conjunctions (
\wedge
) in Eqs.(
46
)–(
47
) implies that the compatibility condition between ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ (Eq.(
44
)) must be satisfied for joint and conditional probabilities to be defined without contradiction.

Notably, the above definition allows for joint probabilities to be defined for a broader range of propositions than in conventional theory.
In the standard formulation of quantum theory, Nelson’s theorem states that a joint probability of two observables
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
can only be defined if and only if the corresponding operators are commutative with each other (i.e.,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}\hat{\mathcal{B}}=\hat{\mathcal{B}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}
)
[
9
, §2.1.4]
.
Correspondingly, the von Neumann-Lüders’ theory
[
10
,
62
]
and the Consistent Histories approach
[
11
, (10.44),§4.6]
require the commutativity of projection operators for the existence of joint probabilities.
In contrast, our theory allows us to define joint probabilities even if partial characteristic operators do not commute.
To see this, let us rewrite the compatibility condition in the following form

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\Big({\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})-{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})\Big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]=0.

(50)

Distinguishing the two cases where the compatibility condition holds, namely

Type-I:

\displaystyle\Big({\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})\Big)^{\dagger}{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})=\Big({\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})\Big)^{\dagger}{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}),

(51)

Type-II:

\displaystyle\Big({\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})\Big)^{\dagger}{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})\neq\Big({\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})\Big)^{\dagger}{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}),

(52)

we see that conventional theories have only considered Type-I compatibility due to the requirement of the commutativity of projection operators (i.e.,
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})\,{=}\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})
).
As is well known, this requirement is based on the premise that the definition of joint probabilities should not be situation-dependent
[
9
]
.
On the other hand, our theory abandons this implicit assumption, allowing Type-II compatibility to enable the situation-dependent definition of joint probabilities.

It is important to note that Assumptions A1–A8 contain neither the consistency condition
[
11
]
nor the decoherence condition
[
56
,
57
]
.
This is because the compatibility condition guarantees the consistency of Boolean logic operations in a different way than in previous theories.
In our theory, it is no longer necessary to link the observation of individual events with the collapse of the density operator.
Thus, off-diagonal elements of the density operator vanish only by the intentional modification of the statistical ensemble (see Eq.(
49
)).
This is made possible by distinguishing between the world of reality, in which individual events occur, and the world of expectation, in which the laws of quantum theory holds.

3.4
Deterministic relations between individual events

To see the important role played by the compatibility condition, it is crucial to identify the meaning of the statistical concepts used in the explanation.
The following definitions are classical but are explicitly written down for later use.

Definition

(Independence)
.

Propositions ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ are
independent
if the conditional probabilities equal the marginal probabilities, namely

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}|\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}),

(53)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}).

(54)

This means that the joint probability of independent propositions can always be factorized into marginal probabilities so that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}\,{\wedge}\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})={\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\,{\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}).

(55)

Definition

(Exclusivity)
.

Propositions ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ are
exclusive
if their joint probability is zero, such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}\,{\wedge}\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\equiv\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big\rangle=0.

(56)

This implies that the conjunction of exclusive propositions is always false:

\displaystyle x^{(j)}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}\,{\wedge}\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})=0.

(57)

It then follows from Eq.(
57
) and the laws of Boolean logic (Assumption A4) that the logical variables of mutually exclusive propositions are mutually additive:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\vee$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})+{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}).

(58)

Definition

(Deterministic Relations)
.

A
deterministic relation
exists between propositions ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ when their truth values always concide as

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),\quad\forall{j}.

(59)

By the definition of conditional probabilities (Eq.(
18
)), this equation is equivalent to

\displaystyle 1={\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}|\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})={\mathrm{Pr}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'}),

(60)

which can be further rewritten using Eq.(
47
) as

\displaystyle\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle=\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\rangle=\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle.

(61)

According to the above definition, a deterministic relation between ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ should be expressed in the form of Eqs.(
59
)–(
61
).
On the other hand, von Neumann-Lüders’ theory
[
10
]
and the Consistent Histories approach
[
11
]
could not define the left-hand side of Eq.(
61
) when projection operators are not commutative (i.e.,
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})\,{\neq}\,

{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})
).
Consequently, the only relation that could be safely expressed in conventional theories was the equality between expectation values, namely

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\big\rangle=\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\big\rangle.

(62)

This means that the deterministic relation in Eq.(
59
) may represent a law of reality that cannot be expressed in the standard formulation.
Note that the compatibility condition between ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ must hold for the deterministic relation to exist.

Since our logical variables take only two values (0 or 1), it is necessary to check the implications of applying approximations to propositions.
To this end, consider an approximate proposition ‘
\underline{\mathrm{B}}
’ replacing original proposition ‘B’ and let ‘TN’ and ‘FP’ be the true-negative and false-positive type confusions due to the replacement, respectively.
Then, the influence of erroneous determination can be incorporated by rewriting Eq.(
59
) as follows:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\Big(\mbox{$\big(\mbox{$\lnot$`TN'$\wedge$`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}\big)\,{\vee}\,\big(\mbox{`FP'$\wedge\lnot$`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}\big)$}\Big),\quad\forall{j}

(63)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{$\lnot$`TN'})\,{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})+{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`FP'})\,{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{$\lnot$`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'}),\quad\forall{j}.

(64)

Assuming that the probabilities of errorneous determinations are
p_{\mathrm{TN}}
and
p_{\mathrm{FP}}
and that ‘TN’ and ‘FP’ are independent with any other propositions, we have
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`TN'})\,{=}\,\sqrt{p_{\mathrm{TN}}}\,\hat{1}
,
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`FP'})\,{=}\,\sqrt{p_{\mathrm{FP}}}\,\hat{1}
, so that

\displaystyle\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'$\wedge\lnot$`TN'})\rangle

\displaystyle\cong\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle\langle{x}(\mbox{$\lnot$`TN'})\rangle,

(65)

\displaystyle\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'$\wedge$`FP'})\rangle

\displaystyle\cong\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle\langle{x}(\mbox{`FP'})\rangle.

(66)

The use of conjunction can be justified because the compatibility condition between ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’ will give the same conclusion as the compatibility condition between ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’.
Multiplying
{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})
or
{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})
to the both side of Eq.(
64
), taking the expectation value, and substituting Eqs.(
65
)–(
66
) into the resulting equations, we get

\displaystyle\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\langle{x}(\mbox{`FP'})\rangle}{\langle{x}(\mbox{$\lnot$`TN'})\rangle-\langle{x}(\mbox{`FP'})\rangle}\,\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\rangle

(67)

\displaystyle=\langle{x}(\mbox{$\lnot$`TN'})\rangle\,\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle.

(68)

When the probabilities of erroneous determinations are sufficiently small so that
\langle{x}(\mbox{$\lnot$`TN'})\rangle\,{\cong}\,1
,
\langle{x}(\mbox{`FP'})\rangle\,{\cong}\,0
, this equation yields

\displaystyle\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'$\wedge$`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle\cong\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}$'})\rangle\cong\langle{x}(\mbox{`$\underline{\mathrm{B}},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}$'})\rangle.

(69)

Thus, an approximate proposition can be reasonably defined if erroneous determinations are independent of the proposition under consideration and if numerical errors are allowed in evaluating joint and conditional probabilities.
To avoid complications, propositions ‘TN’ and ‘FP’ will be omitted as much as possible in the following.

4
Model of reality

4.1
Overview

In the previous section, we introduced a two-layer theoretical framework where Boolean logical variables describe individual trials and the density operator describes statistical properties.
This dual structure aligns with Einstein’s perspective on quantum theory
[
1
,
2
]
.
On the other hand, subsequent experiments have observed quantum jumps between stationary states in both atomic systems
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
and radiation fields
[
23
,
24
]
.
This indicates the necessity of adopting a stationary-state model of reality
[
45
,
38
]
, not the point-particle model by Einstein
[
1
,
2
]
.
Historically, however, Bohr overemphasized the fact that his atomic model renders the light quantum hypothesis superfluous
[
45
,
49
]
, denying the possibility that quantum theory is a two-layer theory
[
51
]
.
Therefore, we reconcile Einstein’s and Bohr’s claims by using the logical variables described above to represent stationary state occupation.
The list of assumptions has already been presented in Table
2
, so this section describes the details of individual assumptions.

4.2
Definition of stationary states

First, we make the following assumptions to define stationary states in the non-relativistic QED sense
[
16
,
33
,
14
]
.

Assumption B1
Definition of the target system.

The Hamiltonian of the target system is given by

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}

\displaystyle={\hat{\mathsf{W}}}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{I}}\right){\hat{\mathsf{W}}}

(70)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}^{K}\frac{1}{2m_{k}}\|{\hat{\bm{p}}}_{k}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}^{K}\sum_{k^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}{\neq}k}\frac{e_{k}e_{k^{\prime}}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{1}{\left\|\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}-\hat{\bm{r}}_{k^{\prime}}\right\|},

(71)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle=\iiint\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\left[\|\hat{\bm{E}}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}})\|^{2}+{c^{2}}\|\hat{\bm{B}}({\bm{x}})\|^{2}\right]d{\bm{x}},

(72)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{I}}

\displaystyle=-\sum^{K}_{k}\frac{e_{k}}{m_{k}}\hat{\bm{A}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})\cdot{\hat{\bm{p}}}_{k}+\sum^{K}_{k}\frac{e_{k}^{2}}{2m_{k}}\left\|{\hat{\bm{A}}_{\mathrm{T}}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})\right\|^{2}+\sum_{k}^{K}\frac{g_{k}e_{k}}{2m_{k}}{\hat{\bm{s}}}_{k}\cdot\hat{\bm{B}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}),

(73)

where
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}
,
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}
, and
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{I}}
represent the Hamiltonian of the atomic system, radiation field, and interaction, respectively.

\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}
and
\hat{\bm{p}}_{k}
(
1\,{\leqq}\,k\,{\leqq}\,K
) are the position and momentum operators of the
k
-th charged particle, and
\hat{\bm{A}}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}})
and
\hat{\bm{E}}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}})
are the transverse components of the vector potential and electric field (
0\,{=}\,{\bm{\nabla}}\cdot\hat{\bm{A}}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}})\,{=}\,{\bm{\nabla}}\cdot\hat{\bm{E}}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}})
), respectively.
The commutation relations are given by

\displaystyle\Big[\hat{r}_{k\xi},\,\hat{p}_{k^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\Big]_{-}={\mathrm{i}}\hbar\,\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{\xi\xi^{\prime}},\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,k,k^{\prime}\,{\leqq}\,K,\quad\xi,\xi^{\prime}\,{\in}\,\{x,y,z\},

(74)

\displaystyle\Big[{\hat{E}_{{\mathrm{T}}\xi}}({\bm{x}},t),\,{\hat{A}_{{\mathrm{T}}\xi^{\prime}}}({\bm{x}}^{\prime},t)\Big]_{-}=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\hbar}{\varepsilon_{0}}\,\delta^{{\mathrm{T}}}_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}),\quad\xi,\xi^{\prime}\,{\in}\,\{x,y,z\},

(75)

with
\delta^{{\mathrm{T}}}_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime})
as the transverse delta function
[
16
, Eq.(10.8-15)]
.
The
\hat{\mathsf{W}}
in Eq.(
70
) is a time-independent unitary operator, introduced to take into account the multipolar Hamiltonian
[
16
, §14.1]
and the field Hamiltonian with displaced vacuum.
See the tables in Appendix
C
for the meaning of other symbols.
2
2

2

For clarity, we consider a closed system with periodic boundary conditions imposed on the surface of a large volume
V
. Thus, the transverse delta function can be expressed as

\displaystyle\delta^{{\mathrm{T}}}_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime})=\iiint\left(\delta_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}-\frac{k_{\xi}k_{\xi^{\prime}}}{\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}\right){\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}{\bm{k}}\cdot({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime})}\,\frac{d{\bm{k}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}=\frac{1}{V}\sum_{{\bm{k}}}\left(\delta_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}-\frac{k_{\xi}k_{\xi^{\prime}}}{\|{\bm{k}}\|^{2}}\right){\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}{\bm{k}}\cdot({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime})}

(76)

Note also that the formal expressions
\hat{\bm{B}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})
and
\hat{\bm{A}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})
can be evaluated in the position representation by letting
\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}\,{\to}\,{\bm{r}}_{k}
and
\hat{\bm{p}}_{k}\,{\to}\,{-}{\mathrm{i}}\hbar{\bm{\nabla}}_{k}
. The superscript of the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}
indicates that the time-independent unitary transformation
\hat{\mathsf{W}}
was considered.

Assumption B2
Definition of stationary states.

The stationary states are defined as the orthonormalized eigenstates of the non-interacting Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}\equiv{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}\right){\hat{\mathsf{U}}}

(77)

by the eigenequation

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}|\phi\rangle=E(\phi)|\phi\rangle,\quad\langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}.

(78)

Here,
\phi
denotes a complete set of quantum numbers,
E(\phi)
is the corresponding eigenvalue, and
\delta_{\phi\phi^{\prime}}
is a Kronecker delta or a delta function, depending on the type of stationary states
[
14
]
.

\hat{\mathsf{U}}
is a time-independent unitary operator, introduced to include coherent states, or more generally, quantum states outside of Hilbert space.

\hat{\mathsf{U}}
may include
{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}
when
{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}\,{\neq}\,\hat{1}
.

Assumption B3
Definition of the set of statoinary states.

The set of orthonormalized stationary states satisfying the given condition
{\mathrm{C}}
is denoted by
3
3

3

For example, the stationary states of a free electron are labeled by
\phi\,{=}\,(p_{x},p_{y},p_{z},\sigma_{\xi})
, where
(p_{x},p_{y},p_{z})\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is a momentum vector and
\sigma_{\xi}\,{\in}\,\{+1/2,-1/2\}
is a spin in the
\xi
direction. Then, a set of stationary states having a particular eigenenergy
{E}
can be expressed as
\{\!\!\{{E}\}\!\!\}\,{\equiv}\,\{\,|\phi\rangle\,|\,\phi\,{=}

\,(p_{E}\cos{\theta},\,p_{E}\sin{\theta}\cos{\phi},\,p_{E}\sin{\theta}\sin{\phi},\sigma_{\xi}),\,p_{E}\,{\equiv}\,\sqrt{2m_{\mathrm{e}}{E}},\,\theta\,{\in}\,[0,\pi),\,\phi\,{\in}\,[0,2\pi),\sigma_{\xi}\,{\in}\,\{+1/2,-1/2\}\}
.

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}_{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}\equiv\left\{\,\forall|\phi\rangle\,\Big|\penalty\ \hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}|\phi\rangle\,{=}\,E(\phi)|\phi\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}},\penalty\ \mbox{$|\phi\rangle$ satisfies $\mathrm{C}$\,}\right\}.

(79)

In particular, given a complete set of stationary states,

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{E}}\}\!\!\}_{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}\equiv\left\{\,\forall|\phi\rangle\,\Big|\penalty\ \hat{H}^{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}|\phi\rangle\,{=}\,E(\phi)|\phi\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}\right\},

(80)

the sets of stationary states that can be expressed in the form

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}_{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}\equiv\Big\{\,\forall|\phi\rangle\,\Big|\,|\phi\rangle\,{\in}\,\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{E}}\}\!\!\}_{\hat{\mathsf{U}}},\,\mbox{$|\phi\rangle$ satisfies $\mathrm{C}^{\,\mu}$\,}\Big\},\quad\mu\in\{1,\ldots,M\}

(81)

are termed as
mutually orthogonalized sets
.
To save symbols, we use the same symbol
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}_{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
to represent the subspace of the Hilbert space spanned by set
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}_{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
.

Because of the time-independent unitary transformation
\hat{\mathsf{U}}
in Eq.(
77
), the stationary states defined by the above assumptions include a wider range of quantum states than the traditional definition
[
14
, Chap.2]
.
However, except in Section
6
, the rest of this paper will not discuss the case
{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}\,{\neq}\,\hat{1}
or
{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}\,{\neq}\,\hat{1}
.
Hence, we tentatively use the conventional definition given below:

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{0}

\displaystyle\equiv\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}},

(82)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{0}|\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=E(\phi)|\phi\rangle,\quad\langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\phi\phi^{\prime}},

(83)

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{\,\forall|\phi\rangle\,\Big|\penalty\ \hat{H}_{0}|\phi\rangle\,{=}\,E(\phi)|\phi\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}},\penalty\ \mbox{$|\phi\rangle$ satisfies $\mathrm{C}$\,}\right\}.

(84)

4.3
Laws of stationary state occupation

Next, following the discussion in Section
3
, we define Boolean logical variables that describe stationary state occupation.

Assumption B4
Definition of elementary propositions.

An elementary proposition is defined as a proposition that represents the occupation of stationary states at a single instant of time.

‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t
’

\displaystyle\equiv\mbox{`A stationary state $|\phi\rangle$ is occupied at time $t$'}\phantom{\Big|}

(85)

‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’

\displaystyle\equiv\mbox{`The stationary states occupied at time $t$ is contained in}

subspace
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}
’

(86)

Assumption B5
Definition of logical variables.

The logical variable describing an elementary proposition is defined by

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$' is True at time $t$}\\
0,&\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$' is False at time $t$}\end{cases},\quad j\in{{\mathbb{N}}},

(87)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$' is True at time $t$}\\
0,&\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$' is False at time $t$}\end{cases},\quad j\in{{\mathbb{N}}},

(88)

where
j
is the trial number and
\mathbb{N}
denotes natural numbers.

Assumption B6
Laws of Boolean logic.

Boolean logic operations on elementary propositions are defined by

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{$\lnot$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big)

\displaystyle=1-{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big),

(89)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big)\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big),

(90)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'$\vee$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)
