\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big)+{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big),

(91)

where the symbols
\lnot
,
\wedge
, and
\vee
denote negation (NOT), conjunction (AND), and disjunction (OR), respectively.
These relations shall hold for elementary propositions defined at different times (
t_{1}\,{\neq}\,t_{2}
).

Assumption B7
Exclusivity.

For any orthogonal stationary states
|\phi_{1}\rangle
and
|\phi_{2}\rangle
that satisfy
\langle\phi_{1}|\phi_{2}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}
,

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big)=0

(92)

for
\phi_{1}\,{\neq}\,\phi_{2}
and

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big)

(93)

for
\phi_{1}\,{=}\,\phi_{2}
.

Assumption B8
Definition of compound propositions.

A compound proposition is defined as the conjunction of elementary propositions ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}
’ (
1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I
) specified at different times:

\displaystyle\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\{t\}$'}\equiv\bigwedge^{I}_{i=1}\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}$'},\quad I\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(94)

Here,
\{t\}
denotes the set of times
t_{1},\ldots,t_{I}
.
For simplicity, the elementary propositions are assumed to be numbered in ascending order of time (
t_{i}\,{<}\,t_{i+1}
).
By definition, elementary propositions can always be expressed as

\displaystyle\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}$'}=\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{i}$'},

(95)

but the sets
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}\}\!\!\}
are not necessarily orthogonalized with each other.
4
4

4

Writing explicitly,
\langle\phi_{i^{\prime}}|\phi_{i^{\prime\prime}}\rangle
is always 0 if
|\phi_{i^{\prime}}\rangle
and
|\phi^{\prime\prime}\rangle
are both in
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
, but not necessarily if
|\phi_{i^{\prime}}\rangle
is in
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
while
|\phi_{i^{\prime\prime}}\rangle
is not.

It is worth noting that the exclusivity assumption (Assumption B7) is necessary to maintain the consistency of the entire set of assumptions (See §
A.1
) and must be distinguished from the Pauli exclusion principle.
Accepting the exclusivity assumption, we have

\displaystyle\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}=\bigvee_{|\phi\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}}\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}

(96)

where
\bigvee_{|\phi\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}}
stands for the conjunction over all stationary states in
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}
.

4.4
Laws of quantum theory

Now, the laws of quantum theory must be restated to allow for the calculation of expectation values.
In conventional theories, the probabilities of compound propositions can be calculated only if all sets
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
(
1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I
) are orthogonalized with each other
[
11
]
.
In contrast, we assume that the probabilities of compound propositions can be calculated as long as the compatibility condition is satisfied, regardless of whether the sets
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
are mutually orthogonalized.
Specific assumptions are as follows:

Assumption B9
Definition of expectation values.

The expectation value of the logical variable is defined by

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\big\rangle\equiv\frac{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'}}{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})}{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'}}\,1},

(97)

where the sums on the right-hand side shall be taken over the ensemble specified by the proposition ‘
\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}
’ and the density operator
\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})
.
For brevity, we omit the limit symbol
\lim_{N\to\infty}
because the timing of taking the statistical limit is obvious in this paper.

Assumption B10
Born rule (1).

The probability of a compound proposition ‘A,
\{t\}
can be calculated by the trace formula of quantum theory, namely

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\big\rangle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big].

(98)

Here, the density operator
\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})
is Hermitian and the characteristic operator
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})
can be expressed as

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})={\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})

(99)

using a partial characteristic operator
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})
.

Assumption B11
Born rule (2).

The partial characteristic operator of an elementary proposition is a projection operator onto a stationary state or a subspace of the Hilbert space spanned by a set of stationary states:

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)

\displaystyle=|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|,

(100)

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\phi\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}}|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|.

(101)

Assumption B12
Conversion rules.

Boolean logic operations transform the characteristic operators of elementary propositions as follows:

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{$\lnot$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big)

\displaystyle=\hat{1}-{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big),

(102)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}::{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big)\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)::,

(103)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'$\vee$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)

\displaystyle={\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big)+{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big),

(104)

where the time-ordering and normal-ordering symbols are defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}::{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big)\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)::

(105)

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big),&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!t_{1}>t_{2},\\
{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big),&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!t_{1}<t_{2},\\
\Big({\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big)&\\
\penalty\ +{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{2}$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{1}$'}\big)\Big)/2,&\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!t_{1}=t_{2}.\end{cases}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

Assumption B13
Equation of motion.

The partial characteristic operator of an elementary proposition evolves in time according to

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)\,\hat{U}(t),

(106)

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big)\,\hat{U}(t),

(107)

where the time evolution operator
\hat{U}(t)
is defined by

\displaystyle\hat{U}(t)\equiv\exp\left(\frac{t}{{\rm{i}}\hbar}\hat{H}\right).

(108)

Assumption B14
Comatibility condition.

The elementary propositions ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}
’

(
1\,{\leqq}\,{i}\,{\leqq}\,I
), used to define a compound proposition ‘
\mathrm{A},\{t\}
’, shall be chosen to satisfy the compatibility conditions, namely

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}(t)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}(t){}^{\dagger}\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}(t)\right)\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}(t)\right)^{\dagger}\Big],\quad 2\,{\leqq}\,\mathcal{I}\,{\leqq}\,I,

(109)

where the chain operator
\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}(t)
for
i_{1}\,{<}\,{\ldots}\,{<}\,i_{\mathcal{I}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}(t)\equiv\lim_{t_{i_{1}}\,{\to}\,t}\cdots\lim_{t_{i_{\mathcal{I}}}\,{\to}\,t}{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i_{1}},t_{i_{1}}$'})\cdots{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i_{\mathcal{I}}},t_{i_{\mathcal{I}}}$'}),\quad 2\,{\leqq}\,\mathcal{I}\,{\leqq}\,I,

(110)

and
{\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}(t)
is an operator obtained by rearranging the partial characteristic operators constituting
\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}(t)
in an arbitrary order.

Since the compatibility condition depends only on a single time variable, it can also be expressed as

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}{}^{\dagger}\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)^{\dagger}\Big],\quad 2\,{\leqq}\,\mathcal{I}\,{\leqq}\,I

(111)

where the density operator in the Schrödinger picture and the time-independent chain operator are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})

\displaystyle\equiv\hat{U}(t)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t),

(112)

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i_{1}}$'})\cdots{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i_{\mathcal{I}}}$'}).

(113)

4.5
Role of compatibility conditions

To elucidate the physical meaning of the aforementioned assumptions, it is beneficial to introduce the following additional assumption:

Assumption B0
Model of reality.

In the complete description based on the Hamiltonians of the entire system, only one stationary state
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
is occupied at any given moment:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi,\Omega^{(j)}(t)},\quad{\forall}j.

(114)

Correspondingly, a logical variable describing the occupation of the set
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}
containing
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
always has a definite value 1.

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)=1\quad\mbox{if\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ $\Omega^{(j)}(t)\,{\in}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}$},\quad{\forall}j.

(115)

When Eq.(
114
) is combined with Assumptions B1–B14,
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
undergoes stochastic evolution over time.
Consequently, our theory exhibits non-deterministic behavior in the sharp representation of Eq.(
114
).
Conversely, in the non-sharp representation of Eq.(
115
), deterministic relations between logical variables can emerge when the set
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}
is sufficiently large to encompass the variation of
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
.
This characteristic allows our theory to exhibit deterministic behavior when employing a non-sharp representation.

To highlight the distinction between the compatibility condition and the consistency condition, we can rewrite the compatibility condition as

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Bigg[\,\Bigg(\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}^{\dagger}\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}-\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)^{\dagger}\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)\bigg)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Bigg]=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ 2\,{\leqq}\,\mathcal{I}\,{\leqq}\,I

(116)

and redefine the Type-I and Type-II compatibility by the following conditions:

Type-I:

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}^{\dagger}\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}=\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)^{\dagger}\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ 2\,{\leqq}\,\mathcal{I}\,{\leqq}\,I,

(117)

Type-II:

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}^{\dagger}\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\neq\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)^{\dagger}\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ 2\,{\leqq}\,\mathcal{I}\,{\leqq}\,I.

(118)

Given that
{\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}
represents an arbitrary permutation of
\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}
, it becomes evident that any theory demanding commutativity of partial characteristic operators permits only Type-I compatibility.
In particular, the Consistent Histories approach states that any meaningful History must satisfy the consistency condition:

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\left[\,\hat{K}({\mathrm{A}}_{1},t_{1};\ldots;{\mathrm{A}}_{I},t_{I})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{K}({\mathrm{A}}^{\prime}_{1},t_{1};\ldots;{\mathrm{A}}^{\prime}_{I},t_{I})^{\dagger}\,\right]=0.

(119)

Practically, this condition is satisfied only when projection operators are commutative with each other

[
11
, Chap.10]
.
Thus, the Consistent Histories approach and the von Neumann-Lüders theory only consider the Type-I compatibility.
On the other hand, assuming a compatibility condition instead of a consistency condition would allow the Type-II compatibility to be considered, albeit at the cost of permitting a situation-dependent definition of joint probabilities.
In this sense, the compatibility condition (Eq.(
116
)) is a generalization of the consistency condition of the Consistent Histories approach.

It is crucial to acknowledge the distinct ways in which compatibility conditions are satisfied in sharp and non-sharp representations.
In the sharp representation of Eq.(
114
), the second-order compatibility condition between elementary proposition takes the following form:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\left[\,\Big({\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}})$'}\big){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}})$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}})$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}})$'}\big)\right.

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\left.{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}})$'}\big){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}})$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}})$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}})$'}\big)\Big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\right]

\displaystyle=\left|\langle\phi_{1}|\phi_{2}\rangle\right|^{2}\Big(\langle\phi_{1}|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\phi_{1}\rangle-\langle\phi_{2}|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\phi_{2}\rangle\Big).

(120)

This condition is satisfied only if the stationary states
|\phi_{1}\rangle
and
|\phi_{2}\rangle
are orthogonal with each other (apart from coincidence).
Thus, the Type-I compatibility is sufficient when the representation is sharp.
On the other hand, in the non-sharp representation of Eq.(
115
), the second-order compatibility condition has the following form:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\left[\,\Big({\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big)\right.

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\left.{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big)\Big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\right]

\displaystyle=\sum^{\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\}\!\!\}}_{|\phi_{1}\rangle}\sum^{\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\}\!\!\}}_{|\phi_{2}\rangle}\left|\langle\phi_{1}|\phi_{2}\rangle\right|^{2}\Big(\langle\phi_{1}|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\phi_{1}\rangle-\langle\phi_{2}|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\phi_{2}\rangle\Big).

(121)

At first glance, it is not obvious that this expression can be satisfied for reasons other than the commutativity of partial characteristic operators
[
11
]
.
Nevertheless, we can show that the compatibility condition always holds among elementary propositions satisfying Eq.(
115
) (i.e.,
{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'})\,{=}\,1
).
The proof is simple and is shown in §
B.1
.
In this respect, the compatibility condition is a condition that determines the validity of approximate representations of reality.

5
Key features of our model

Next, we summarize the key properties derived from the above assumptions.
First, we illustrate how to describe degenerate systems and composite systems, demonstrating that deterministic relations are established between entangled subsystems.
Second, we define the instantaneous values of physical quantities in individual trials, showing that the compatibility condition limits the possibility of arithmetic operations between instantaneous values.
Third, we formulate a classical approximation for the instantaneous values of physical quantities, deriving the complementarity principle and classical equations of motion.
Fourth, by focusing on entangled spin systems, we show how our theory avoids the contradiction caused by the Bell and CHSH inequalities.

5.1
Deterministic relations between entangled subsystems

5.1.1
Representation of degenerate system

Let us first look at a simple example to see how the compatibility condition adequately captures the constraints imposed on degenerate systems.
For this purpose, consider a spin-1/2 system with zero magnetic field and let
|{\uparrow}_{\xi}\rangle
and
|{\downarrow}_{\xi}\rangle
be two stationary states with
\xi
(
{\in}\{x,y,z\}
) as the measurement direction.
In this example, the stationary states cannot be uniquely determined due to degeneracy.
However, if we define elementary propositions by

‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{\xi}}\}}})
’

\displaystyle\equiv\mbox{`$|{\uparrow}_{\xi}\rangle$ is occupied'},\quad\xi\in\{x,y,z\}

(122)

‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{\xi}}\}}})
’

\displaystyle\equiv\mbox{`$|{\downarrow}_{\xi}\rangle$ is occupied'},\quad\xi\in\{x,y,z\}

(123)

and use a concrete form of partial characteristic operators, namely

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{\xi}}\}}})$'})=|{\uparrow}_{\xi}\rangle\langle{\uparrow}_{\xi}|,\quad{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{\xi}}\}}})$'})=|{\downarrow}_{\xi}\rangle\langle{\downarrow}_{\xi}|,\quad\xi\in\{x,y,z\},

(124)

we can determine whether or not the given set of propositions satisfies the compatibility condition.
It is easy to see that the compatibility conditions hold among
5
5

5

The compatibility condition always holds between ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{E}}\}\!\!\}})
’ and any proposition if
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{E}}\}\!\!\}})$'})\,{=}\,\hat{1}
. This is why Eq.(
125
) and Eq.(
126
) contain ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{x}}\}}})
’
{\vee}
‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{x}}\}}})
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{y}}\}}})
’
{\vee}
‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{y}}\}}})
’. Note also that
x^{(j)}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{x}}\}}})$'}{\vee}\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{x}}\}}})$'})\,{=}\,1
and
x^{(j)}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{y}}\}}})$'}{\vee}\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{y}}\}}})$'})\,{=}\,1
.

\displaystyle\big\{\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{x}}\}}})$'},\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{x}}\}}})$'},\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{x}}\}}})$'${\vee}$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{x}}\}}})$'},\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{y}}\}}})$'${\vee}$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{y}}\}}})$'}\,\big\}

(125)

and among

\displaystyle\big\{\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{y}}\}}})$'},\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{y}}\}}})$'},\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{x}}\}}})$'${\vee}$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{x}}\}}})$'},\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{y}}\}}})$'${\vee}$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{y}}\}}})$'}\,\big\},

(126)

but not for the set that involves propositions with different measurement directions, e.g.,

\displaystyle\big\{\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{x}}\}}})$'},\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{y}}\}}})$'}\,\big\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\big\{\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\uparrow}_{x}}\}}})$'},\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\downarrow}_{y}}\}}})$'}\,\big\}.

(127)

Therefore, there is no conjunction and no joint probability for propositions describing different measurement directions.
This suggests that the instantaneous value of a particular component of the spin angular momentum must be defined using only propositions with the same measurement direction.
From a comparison with the quantum theoretical formulas for their expectation values, the only possibility is

\displaystyle S^{(j)}_{\xi}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar}{2}x^{(j)}\Big(\mbox{`o(${\uparrow}_{\xi}$),\,$t$'}\Big)-\frac{\hbar}{2}x^{(j)}\Big(\mbox{`o(${\downarrow}_{\xi}$),\,$t$'}\Big),\quad\xi\in\{x,y,z\}.

(128)

Similarly, simultaneous determination of the instantaneous values of
S^{(j)}_{x}(t)
and
S^{(j)}_{y}(t)
is impossible due to the lack of a conjunction of underlying propositions.
This is exactly what is expected for a spin-1/2 system.

Incidentally, if
\{\!\!\{{{E}_{0}}\}\!\!\}
is the set of all stationary states with a given energy
{E}_{0}
and if
|{E}_{1},\chi\rangle
is another stationary state with eigenenergy
{E}_{1}
and a degeneracy index
\chi
, then the compatibility condition always holds between ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{E}_{0}}\}\!\!\}})
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{E}_{1},\chi}\}}})
’.
For example, the second-order compatibility condition takes the following form:

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\left[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{E}_{0}}\}\!\!\}})$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{E}_{1},\chi}\}}})$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{E}_{1},\chi}\}}})$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{E}_{0}}\}\!\!\}})$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\left[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{E}_{1},\chi}\}}})$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{E}_{0}}\}\!\!\}})$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{E}_{0}}\}\!\!\}})$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{E}_{1},\chi}\}}})$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\right].

(129)

The proof is self-explanatory due to the orthogonality of stationary states (Assumption B2).
This result is useful when calculating the light detection probability (cf. Supplementary Material B and Ref.
[
15
]
).

5.1.2
Representation of non-interacting subsystems

Next, we define the logical variables of subsystems for a system consisting of non-interacting subsystems.
To do this, suppose that the non-interacting Hamiltonian can be approximated by the effective expression

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{0}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\hat{H}_{0}^{(l)},

(130)

and that the stationary state
|\phi^{(l)}\rangle^{\!l}
of the
l
-th subsystem is defined by

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{0}^{(l)}|\phi^{(l)}\rangle^{\!l}={E}^{(l)}(\phi^{(l)})\,|\phi^{(l)}\rangle^{\!l},\quad{}^{l}\!\langle{\phi}^{(l)}|\phi^{\prime(l)}\rangle^{\!l}=\delta_{{\phi}^{(l)},{\phi^{\prime}}^{(l)}},\quad 1\leqq l\leqq L.

(131)

Here,
\hat{H}_{0}^{(l)}
is the Hamiltoian of the
l
-th subsystem, and
\phi^{(l)}
denotes the quantum number of the
l
-th subsystem.
Then, the stationary state
|\phi\rangle
of the entire system can be expressed as a tensor product, namely

\displaystyle|\phi\rangle=|\phi^{(1)}\ldots\phi^{(L)}\rangle\equiv|\phi^{(1)}\rangle^{\!1}{\otimes}\cdots{\otimes}|\phi^{(L)}\rangle^{\!L},

(132)

and from Eq.(
132
) and the Born rule (Assumption B11), we obtain

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)

\displaystyle=\prod^{L}_{l=1}{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{$\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}})$'}$}\big),\quad{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{$\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}})$'}$}\big)=|\phi^{(l)}\rangle^{\!l}{}^{l}\!\langle\phi^{(l)}|,

(133)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)

\displaystyle=\prod^{L}_{l=1}{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{$\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}})$'}$}\big),\quad{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{$\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}})$'}$}\big)=|\phi^{(l)}\rangle^{\!l}{}^{l}\!\langle\phi^{(l)}|.

(134)

Here, ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}})
’ represents the proposition ‘A stationary state
|\phi^{(l)}\rangle^{\!l}
of the
l
-th subsystem is occupied’.
It then follows from the commutativity of
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}})$'})
that compatibility conditions are satisfied between propositions describing different subsystems.
Accordingly, Eqs.(
133
)–(
134
) can be rewritten using the conjunction of
L
propositioins as

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)={\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{$\displaystyle\bigwedge^{L}_{l=1}\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}})$'}$}\right),\quad{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)={\hat{x}}\left(\mbox{$\displaystyle\bigwedge^{L}_{l=1}\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}})$'}$}\right).

(135)

Then, the simple lemma presented in Supplementary Material A guarantees that for non-interacting subsystems, the logical variable of the entire system can always be factored into the logical variables of the subsystems as follows:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\left(\mbox{$\displaystyle\bigwedge^{L}_{l=1}\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}}),t$'}$}\right)=\prod^{L}_{l=1}{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{$\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}}),t$'}$}\big).

(136)

On the other hand, the expectation value
\langle{x}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'})\rangle
of the entire system is not necessarily factorized into the expectation value
\langle{x}(\mbox{$\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}}\}^{{l}}}}),t$'}$})\rangle
of the subsystems.
This results from quantum entanglement, which prevents propositions of different subsystems from becoming independent (§
3.3
) due to interaction at an earlier time.

5.1.3
Deterministic relations in entangled subsystems

To see this concretely, let us see how entanglement is described using the logic variables of subsystems.
For simplicity, consider a system consisting of two subsystems and assume that the interaction is negligible for
t\,{\geqq}\,t_{0}
, but not necessarily for
t\,{<}\,t_{0}
.
Additionally, we assume that for
t\,{\geqq}\,t_{0}
, each subsystem has degenerate stationary states
\{|\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}\rangle\,|\,\chi_{1}\,{\in}\,{\mathsf{K}}_{1}\}
and
\{|\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}\rangle\,|\,\chi_{2}\,{\in}\,{\mathsf{K}}_{2}\}
, with
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
as degenerate indices, and that the system is entangled at least at
t\,{=}\,t_{0}
.
In this case, the stationary state of the entire system is generally of the form

\displaystyle|\phi\rangle=\sum_{\chi_{1}}\sum_{\chi_{2}}c_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}}|\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}\rangle^{\!1}{\otimes}|\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}\rangle^{\!2},\quad\sum_{\chi_{1}}\sum_{\chi_{2}}|c_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}}|^{2}=1,

(137)

but by the assumption of entanglement, the density operator of the entire system has the following form:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{entangled},t_{0}$'})=\big|\phi_{\mathrm{entangled}}\big\rangle\big\langle\phi_{\mathrm{entangled}}\big|,\quad t>t_{0},

(138)

where
|\phi_{\mathrm{entangled}}\rangle
is a stationary state defined by

\displaystyle|\phi_{\mathrm{entangled}}\rangle\equiv\sum_{\chi}c_{\chi}|\phi^{(1)}_{\chi}\rangle^{\!1}{\otimes}|\phi^{(2)}_{\chi}\rangle^{\!2},\quad\sum_{\chi}|c_{\chi}|^{2}=1.

(139)

Under these assumptions, the logical variables of subsystems can be defined consistently for
t\,{\geqq}t_{0}
because there is no interaction.
Using the specific form of partial characteristic operators, namely

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{$\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}}\}^{{1}}}})$'}$}\big)=|\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}\rangle^{\!1}{}^{1}\!\langle\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}|,\quad{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{$\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}}\}^{{2}}}})$'}$}\big)=|\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}\rangle^{\!2}{}^{2}\!\langle\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}|,

(140)

we can show that the compatibility condition is satisfied between propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}}\}^{{1}}}})
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}}\}^{{2}}}})
’.
Furthermore, the joint probability of ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}}\}^{{1}}}})
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}}\}^{{2}}}})
’ can be calculated using Eq.(
138
) and Eq.(
140
) as

\displaystyle\Big\langle x\Big({\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}}\}^{{1}}}}),t$'}}{\wedge}{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}}\}^{{2}}}}),t$'}}\Big)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\Big[\,::\hat{x}\Big({\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}}\}^{{1}}}})$'}}\Big)\,\hat{x}\Big({\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}}\}^{{2}}}})$'}}\Big)::\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

(141)

\displaystyle={}^{1}\!\langle\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}|{}^{2}\!\langle\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}|\Bigg(\sum_{\chi}c_{\chi}|\phi^{(1)}_{\chi}\rangle_{1}{\otimes}|\phi^{(2)}_{\chi}\rangle_{2}\Bigg)\Bigg(\sum_{\chi^{\prime}}c^{*}_{\chi^{\prime}}{}^{1}\!\langle\phi^{(1)}_{\chi^{\prime}}|{\otimes}{}^{2}\!\langle\phi^{(2)}_{\chi^{\prime}}|\Bigg)|\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}\rangle^{\!1}|\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}\rangle^{\!2}

(142)

\displaystyle=\big|c_{\chi}\big|^{2}\delta_{\chi,\chi_{1}}\delta_{\chi,\chi_{2}}.

(143)

The merginal probabilities can similarly be computed as

\displaystyle\Big\langle x\Big({\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}_{\chi_{l}}}\}^{{l}}}}),t$'}}\Big)\Big\rangle=\mathrm{Tr}\Big[\,\hat{x}\Big({\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(l)}_{\chi_{l}}}\}^{{l}}}})$'}}\Big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

\displaystyle=\big|c_{\chi_{l}}\big|^{2},\quad l\in\{1,2\}.

(144)

Combining Eq.(
143
) and Eq.(
144
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\langle x\Big({\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}}\}^{{1}}}}),t$'}}{\wedge}{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}}\}^{{2}}}}),t$'}_{2}}\Big)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big\langle x\Big({\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(1)}_{\chi_{1}}}\}^{{1}}}}),t$'}}\Big)\Big\rangle\delta_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle x\Big({\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(2)}_{\chi_{2}}}\}^{{2}}}}),t$'}_{2}}\Big)\Big\rangle\delta_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}},

(145)

so that for
\chi_{1}\,{=}\,\chi_{2}
,

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(1)}_{\chi}}\}^{{1}}}})$'}\right)={x^{(j)}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi^{(2)}_{\chi}}\}^{{2}}}})$'}\right),\quad\forall\chi,\quad\forall j.

(146)

In the standard formulation of quantum theory, deterministic relations between entangled subsystems have conventionally been explained based on the collapse of density operators or state vectors
[
63
]
.
By contrast, the above calculation shows that the collapse of state vectors is unnecessary to establish deterministic relations in entangled systems.
This conclusion was reached because our theory distinguished between the world of reality and the world of expectation, decoupling the time evolution of the density operator from the occurrence of individual events.
The emergence of the deterministic relations is due to the specific form of the density operator in Eq.(
138
).
If the interaction is non-negligible and the stationary states of subsystems are not well-defined, a more accurate description based on the stationary state of the entire system is required.

5.2
Instantaneous values of physical quantities

5.2.1
Basic definitions

Our model of reality (Assumption B0) requires that instantaneous values of physical quantities in individual trials be defined based on the occupation of stationary states.
In other words, for a physical quantity
F
to have a definite value in each stationary state, the corresponding operator
\hat{F}
must commute with the non-interacting Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{0}
(Eq.(
82
)).
Therefore, we begin by considering physical quantities
F
where the operator
\hat{F}
satisfies
[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}]_{-}\,{=}\,0
, and demonstrate that its instantaneous value can be defined rationally in both statistical and quantum-theoretical sense.
In this case, we can choose stationary state
|\phi\rangle
as a simultaneous eigenstate of
\hat{F}
and
\hat{H}_{0}
, and express the spectral decomposition
[
9
]
of
\hat{F}
and
\hat{H}_{0}
as

\displaystyle\hat{F}=\sum_{|\phi\rangle}{F}(\phi)\,|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|,\quad\hat{H}_{0}=\sum_{|\phi\rangle}E(\phi)\,|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|,\quad\hat{1}=\sum_{|\phi\rangle}|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|.

(147)

Here,
F(\phi)\,{=}\,\langle\phi|\hat{F}|\phi\rangle
and
E(\phi)\,{=}\,\langle\phi|\hat{H}_{0}|\phi\rangle
due to the orthogonality of the stationary states (Assumption B2).
The range notation is omitted for the sums over the complete set of stationary states.
If we define the instantaneous value of
F
as

\displaystyle F^{(j)}(t)\equiv\sum_{|\phi\rangle}F(\phi)\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big),\quad F(\phi)\,{\equiv}\,\langle\phi|\hat{F}|\phi\rangle,\quad\forall j,

(148)

its expectation value satisfies

\displaystyle\langle F(t)\rangle=\sum_{|\phi\rangle}F(\phi)\,\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)\big\rangle,

(149)

while Assumptions B1–B14 yield

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'}}{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)}{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{`\scriptsize${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'}}1}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\right],

(150)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle=|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|.

(151)

Substituting Eqs.(
150
)–(
151
) into Eq.(
149
) and using the first equation of Eq.(
147
), we find that the expectation value of
F
, defined in the statistical sense, can be correctly calculated using the trace formula of quantum theory:

\displaystyle\langle F(t)\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'}}F^{(j)}(t)}{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{`\scriptsize${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'}}1}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\,\hat{F}\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\right].

(152)

(a)
{F}
{F}_{-2}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{-2}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{-1}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{-1}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{0}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{0}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{1}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{1}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{2}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{2}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{3}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{3}\}\!\!\}
(b)
{F}
{F}_{-2}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{-2}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{-1}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{-1}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{0}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{0}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{1}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{1}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{2}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{2}\}\!\!\}
{F}_{3}
\{\!\!\{{F}_{3}\}\!\!\}

Figure 3:
Discretization grids for
F
with (a) uniform and (b) non-uniform grid intervals.

5.2.2
Definition of instantaneous values:
\big[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}\,{=}\,0

To define arithmetic operations between instantaneous values of two or more physical quantities, it is useful to introduce a non-sharp representation that does not distinguish between stationary states with the same value of
F
.

Definition C1
Set of stationary states with specified value and precision of a physical quantity.

For an operator
\hat{F}
that satisfies

\displaystyle\big[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}=0,

(153)

the set of stationary states with physical quantity
F
having value
{F}_{i}
is defined by

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}\equiv\bigg\{\,\forall|\phi\rangle\,\bigg|\,\frac{{F}_{i-1}+{F}_{i}}{2}\,{\leqq}\,F(\phi)\,{<}\,\frac{{F}_{i}+{F}_{i+1}}{2},\penalty\ \hat{F}|\phi\rangle\,{=}\,F(\phi)|\phi\rangle,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \hat{H}_{0}|\phi\rangle\,{=}\,E(\phi)|\phi\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}\,\bigg\},\quad i\in{\mathbb{Z}},

(154)

where the discretization grid of
F
with interval
\{\delta{F}_{i}\,|\,i\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{Z}}\}
is defined by

\displaystyle{F}_{i+1}\equiv{F}_{i}+\delta{F}_{i},\quad F_{0}\equiv 0,\quad i\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(155)

(Fig.
3
).
The interval of the discretization grids, abbreviated as
\delta{F}
, is hereafter referred to as the precision of proposition representation for
F
.
To save symbol, the subspace spanned by set
\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
is also represented by the same symbol
\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
.

According to this definition, proposition ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ can alternatively be expressed as ‘Physical quantity
F
has a value
F_{i}
with precision
\delta{F}
, at time
t
’.
The definition of instantaneous values given in Eq.(
148
) can then be generalized as follows.

Definition C2
Instantaneous value of physical quantity.

For an operator
\hat{F}
that satisfies

\displaystyle\big[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}=0,

(156)

the instantaneous value of
F
in the
j
-th trial is defined by

\displaystyle{F}^{(j)}(t)\equiv\sum_{i}{F}_{i}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j.

(157)

Using this definition, the expectation value of
F
, defined statistically in Eq.(
152
), can be correctly calculated as

\displaystyle\big\langle{F}(t)\big\rangle\cong\sum_{i}{F}_{i}\,\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)\big\rangle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\,\hat{F}\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\right]

(158)

within an approximation that assigns a value
F_{i}
to all stationary states in
\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
:

\displaystyle\langle\phi|\hat{F}|\phi\rangle\,{\cong}\,F_{i},\quad\forall|\phi\rangle\in\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}.

(159)

It is worth noting that Eq.(
157
) and Eq.(
158
) exactly match Eq.(
148
) and Eq.(
152
) when the grid spacings
\{\delta{F}_{i}\,|\,i\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{Z}}\}
approach zero in the continuous part of the eigenvalue spectrum and equals the spectral spacings in the discrete part.
This means that Definition C2 includes the simple case of Eq.(
148
).

Figure 4:
The count number
n(t)
of a photodetector
[
15
]
. Lower left: the instantaneous value of
n(t)
in an individual trial. Lower right: the expectation value of
n(t)
. Upper left: the instantaneous values of logical variables
x^{(j)}(\mbox{`o$(n),t$'})
. Upper right: the expectation values of logical variables
\langle x(\mbox{`o$(n),t$'})\rangle
. To clarify the logical connection with Ref.
[
15
]
, the same figure in Ref.
[
15
]
is used as is. See Ref.
[
15
]
for details of the calculations.

5.2.3
Arithmetic operations between instantaneous values:
0\,{=}\,\big[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}\,{=}\,\big[\hat{G},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}

According to Definition C2, specifying the instantaneous value
F
is equivalent to specifying the instantaneous values of the following logical variables:

\displaystyle\left\{\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)\,\Big|\,i\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,\right\}

(160)

(Fig.
4
, Upper left).
Note that at any given moment, only one of these logical variables will have a value of 1.
The probability distribution of
F
can also be represented by the expectation values of these logical variables, namely

\displaystyle\left\{\,\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)\big\rangle\,\Big|\,i\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,\right\}

(161)

(Fig.
4
, Upper right).
Accordingly, under definitions C1 and C2, both the instantaneous value and probability distribution of
F
can be fully represented by proposition
\big\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}

\,\big|\,i\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,\big\}
alone.
It then follows that arithmetic operations between the instantaneous values of
F
and
G
should also be represented by propositions, specifically the result of logical operations on propositions
\left\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\,\big|\,i\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,\right\}
and
\left\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\,\big|\,i\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,\right\}
.
For later use, we provide the definitions of addition and multiplication of
F
and
G
:

Definition C3
Arithmetic operations between instantaneous values.

Let
\hat{F}
and
\hat{G}
be operators satisfying

\displaystyle 0\,{=}\,\big[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}\,{=}\,\big[\hat{G},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-},

(162)

and let the compatibility conditions be satisfied for all combinations of propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ (
i\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{Z}}
,
i^{\prime}\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{Z}}
).
Then, the addition and multiplication of
F
and
G
can be defined as

\displaystyle({F}+{G})^{(j)}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{i}\sum_{i^{\prime}}\Big({F}_{i}+{G}_{i^{\prime}}\Big)\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'${\wedge}$`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,

(163)

\displaystyle({F}{G})^{(j)}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{i}\sum_{i^{\prime}}\Big({F}_{i}{G}_{i^{\prime}}\Big)\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'${\wedge}$`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j.

(164)

The compatibility conditions between propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)
’ are required because the conjunction (
\wedge
) must exist for the right-hand sides of Eqs.(
163
)–(
164
) to be defined.
When these conditions are satisfied, Eqs.(
163
)–(
164
) can be simplified using the laws of Boolean logic (Assumption B6) as

\displaystyle({F}+{G})^{(j)}(t)

\displaystyle={F}^{(j)}(t)+{G}^{(j)}(t),\quad\forall j,

(165)

\displaystyle({F}{G})^{(j)}(t)

\displaystyle={F}^{(j)}(t)\,{G}^{(j)}(t),\quad\forall j.

(166)

The extension to more general functional operations follows naturally from these definitions.
Moreover, the condition for defining the joint probability of
F
and
G
,
\big\langle x\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}

\mbox{${\wedge}$`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)\big\rangle
, is identical to that in Definition C3.

It is important to note that, according to Definition C3,
[\hat{F},\hat{G}]_{-}\,{\neq}\,0
does not necessarily preclude the existence of a joint probability of
F
and
G
.
More precisely, if
[\hat{F},\hat{G}]_{-}\,{\neq}\,0
, there is no stationary state to which simultaneous values of
F
and
G
can be assigned.
However, if
0\,{=}\,[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}]_{-}\,{=}\,[\hat{G},\hat{H}_{0}]_{-}
, there exist a set of stationary states,
\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
, having a definite value of
F
, and a set of stationary states,
\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}
, having a definite value of
G
.
The compatibility conditions between ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ are then satisfied if

\displaystyle 1=\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)\rangle=\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)\rangle,\quad\forall j.

(167)

This means that in our theory, the arithmetic operations and joint probabilities of
F
and
G
may exist even when
\big[\hat{F},\hat{G}\big]_{-}\,{\neq}\,0
.
This is because, our theory allows for Type-II compatibility through the compatibility condition, whereas conventional theory prohibits Type-II compatibility through the orthogonality or consistency conditions (See §
3.3
or §
4.5
).

5.2.4
Definition of instantaneous values:
\big[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}\,{\neq}\,0
or
\big[\hat{G},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}\,{\neq}\,0

For general physical quantities
F
where the operator
\hat{F}
satisfies the condition
[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}]_{-}\,{\neq}\,0
, no simultaneous eigenstates of
F
and
\hat{H}_{0}
exist.
Consequently, according to our model of reality, it is impossible to define instantaneous values for such physical quantities
F
.
Concrete examples of such quantities include the position
{\bm{r}}
of a charged particle, and the real and imaginary parts of the modal amplitude
\tilde{a}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}
of a radiation field.
Experimentally, however, instead of these quantities, instantaneous values of different physical quantities
\mathcal{F}
are measured in systems that include additional measurement instruments.
For instance, in the measurement of a charged particle’s position
[
64
]
, the instantaneous value of
F\,{=}\,{\bm{r}}
is estimated from the instantaneous value of the position of water droplets
\mathcal{F}\,{=}\,{\mathcal{R}}
in a cloud chamber, using the following empirical formula:

\displaystyle{\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}(t).

(168)

If a magnetic field is applied during measurement, the instantaneous value of the momentum
G\,{=}\,{\bm{p}}
of the charged particle,
{\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)\,{=}\,{\mathcal{P}}^{(j)}(t)
, can be estimated from the radius of curvature of the same water droplet trajectory used for position measurement.
Similarly, in the measurement of radiation field mode amplitudes
[
16
]
, the instantaneous values of the real part
F\,{=}\,{\mathrm{Re}}[\tilde{\alpha}]
and imaginary part
G\,{=}\,{\mathrm{Im}}[\tilde{\alpha}_{\lambda}]
are determined based on the count numbers
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
of two photodetectors constituting a heterodyne photodetector
[
65
,
66
]
, using the following empirical formulas:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Re}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{(j)}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}(t)]={\mathrm{Const.}}\frac{{\mathcal{N}}_{1}^{(j)}(t)+{\mathcal{N}}_{2}^{(j)}(t)}{2},\quad{\mathrm{Im}}[\tilde{\alpha}^{(j)}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}(t)]={\mathrm{Const.}}\frac{{\mathcal{N}}_{1}^{(j)}(t)-{\mathcal{N}}_{2}^{(j)}(t)}{2{\mathrm{i}}}.

(169)

In other words, in experiments, the instantaneous values of unmeasurable physical quantities
F
are determined based on the instantaneous values of physical quantities
\mathcal{F}
in an extended system with an additional measurement instrument.
These empirical formulas can be generally expressed using measurement propositions as follows:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,\quad\forall i,

(170)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,\quad\forall i.

(171)

For this reason, we next consider defining the instantaneous values of physical quantities
F
and
G
based on the instantaneous values of physical quantities
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
using these deterministic relations.

To this end, we first clarify the definition of instantaneous values for
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
in the extended system.
Generally, we consider cases where the operators for
F
and
G
do not commute with the non-interacting Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{0}
while
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be measured by adding measurement instruments.
It then follows from the assumptions that

\displaystyle\big[\hat{F},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}\neq 0\quad\mbox{or}\quad\big[\hat{G},\hat{H}_{0}\big]_{-}\neq 0

(172)

and

\displaystyle\big[\hat{\mathcal{F}},\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{F}}\big]_{-}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\big[\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\big]_{-}=0.

(173)

Note that there is no guarantee that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be measured using common measurement instrument, so we have distinguished between the non-interacting Hamiltonian
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{F}}
describing the measurement of
\mathcal{F}
and
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{G}}
describing the measurement of
\mathcal{G}
.
In general, when the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
cannot be determined in a single trial (i.e., using common measurement instrument), it is impossible to use common non-interacting Hamiltonians, total Hamiltonians, stationary states, and density operators for the measurements of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
(
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{F}}\,{\neq}\,\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{G}}
).
In such cases, the measurements of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
cannot be represented by a common density operator, and consequently, the compatibility conditions between
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
do not hold.
On the other hand, when the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be determined in a single trial, it is possible to use common non-interacting Hamiltonians, total Hamiltonians, stationary states, and density operators for the measurements of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
(
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{F}}\,{=}\,\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\,{\equiv}\,\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
), allowing for the following definition:

Definition C4
Instantaneous values of physical quantities in extended systems (1).

When physical quantities
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be measured simultaneously in a single trial in the extended system with additional measurement instruments, it is possible to use common non-interacting Hamiltonians, total Hamiltonians, stationary states, and density operators for the measurements of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, and the following relationship holds:

\displaystyle 0=\big[\hat{\mathcal{F}},\hat{\mathcal{G}}\big]_{-}=\big[\hat{\mathcal{F}},\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\big]_{-}=\big[\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\big]_{-}.

(174)

In this case, the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be defined by the following equations:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{(j)}(t)=\sum_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,

(175)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}^{(j)}(t)=\sum_{i}{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j.

(176)

In this case, compatibility conditions between ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ hold by assumption, allowing for the definition of arithmetic operations and joint probabilities for instantaneous values
{\mathcal{F}}^{(j)}(t)
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{(j)}(t)
.

Definition C5
Instantaneous values of physical quantities in extended systems (2).

When physical quantities
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
cannot be measured simultaneously in a single trial, due to differences in measurement instruments, it is impossible to use common non-interacting Hamiltonians, total Hamiltonians, stationary states, and density operators for the measurements of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, meaning that

\displaystyle\big[\hat{\mathcal{F}},\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{F}}\big]_{-}=0

\displaystyle\quad\mbox{and}\quad\big[\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{G}}\big]_{-}=0

(177)

with

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{F}}\neq\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{0,\mathcal{G}}.

(178)

In this case, the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can still be defined by the following equations:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{(j)}(t)=\sum_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,

(179)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}^{(j)}(t)=\sum_{i}{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j.

(180)

However, because the compatibility conditions between ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ do not hold, arithmetic operations and joint probabilities for instantaneous values
\mathcal{F}^{(j)}(t)
and
\mathcal{G}^{(j)}(t)
cannot be defined.

5.2.5
Physical quantities as proxy representations

We now define the instantaneous values of physical quantities
F
and
G
in the original system using the deterministic relations in Eqs.(
170
)–(
171
).
Note that these equations merely indicate that the instantaneous values of
F
and
G
in the original system are proxy representations of the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
in the extended system.
Crucially, for this type of proxy representations to faithfully represent the behavior of the original quantities in both individual trials and expectations, the truth values, probability distributions, and joint probabilities of the measurement propositions must all coincide (cf. §
5.2.3
).
Therefore, we define physical quantities as proxy representations as follows:

Definition C6
Complete proxy representation.

When the instantaneous values of physical quantities
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be measured simultaneously in a single trial in the extended system including measurement instruments (Definition C4), we define propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ in the idealized system without measurement instruments to be complete proxy representations of propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ in the extended system including measurement instruments if the following relations hold:

(Equivalence of truth values)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,\quad\forall i

(181)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,\quad\forall i

(182)

(Equivalence of probability distributions)

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\hat{\wp}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big],\quad\forall i

(183)

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\hat{\wp}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big],\quad\forall i

(184)

(Equivalence of joint probabilities in the simultaneous limit)

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\hat{\wp}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big],

\displaystyle\quad\forall i,\penalty\ \forall i^{\prime}

(185)

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\hat{\wp}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big],

\displaystyle\quad\forall i,\penalty\ \forall i^{\prime}

(186)

For simplicity of notation, we assume that the indices
i
and
i^{\prime}
of the sets have been adjusted so that the deterministic relations in Eqs.(
181
)–(
182
) hold.
When these conditions are satisfied,
F
and
G
are expressed as complete proxy representations of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition C7
Incomplete proxy representation.

When the instantaneous values of physical quantities
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
cannot be measured simultaneously in a single trial in the extended system including measurement instruments (Definition C5), we define propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ in the idealized system without measurement instruments to be incomplete proxy representations of propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ in the extended system including measurement instruments if the following equations hold:

(Equivalence of truth values)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,\quad\forall i

(187)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,\quad\forall i

(188)

(Equivalence of probability distributions)

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\hat{\wp}_{\mathcal{F}}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big],\quad\forall i

(189)

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\hat{\wp}_{\mathcal{G}}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right)$'}\right)\,\Big],\quad\forall i

(190)

Here, considering the differences in measurement instruments, we denote the density operator describing the measurement of
\mathcal{F}
in the extended system as
\hat{\wp}_{\mathcal{F}}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})
, and the density operator describing the measurement of
\mathcal{G}
as
\hat{\wp}_{\mathcal{G}}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})
.
When these conditions are satisfied,
F
and
G
are expressed as incomplete proxy representations of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition C8
Instantaneous values of physical quantities as proxy representations.

When propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ are complete proxy representations of propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{G}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’, we define the instantaneous values of physical quantities
F
and
G
by the following equations:

\displaystyle{F}^{(j)}(t)=\sum_{i}{F}_{i}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,

(191)

\displaystyle{G}^{(j)}(t)=\sum_{i}{G}_{i}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big),\quad\forall j.

(192)

5.2.6
Differences from conventional theory

With the definitions provided above, arithmetic operations and joint probabilities between instantaneous values of
F
and
G
can be defined not when
[\hat{F},\hat{G}]_{-}\,{=}\,0
, but when the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be measured in a single trial.
Specifically, when the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be measured in a single trial, the compatibility conditions between
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
hold according to Definition C4, and the compatibility conditions between
F
and
G
hold according to Definition C6.
Therefore, in this case, arithmetic operations and joint probabilities can be defined between the instantaneous values of both
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, and
F
and
G
.
Conversely, when the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
cannot be measured in a single trial, the compatibility conditions between
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
do not hold according to Definition C5, and the compatibility conditions between
F
and
G
do not hold according to Definition C7.
Consequently, in this case, the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
,
F
, and
G
can be defined according to Definitions C5 and C7, but arithmetic operations and joint probabilities between the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, or
F
and
G
, cannot be defined.

It is important to note that this conclusion differs from conventional theory.
To see this, we focus on two typical cases that satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle\big[\hat{F},\hat{G}\big]_{-}\neq 0.

(193)

In this case, Nelson’s theorem in the standard formulation states that the joint probability of
F
and
G
is not definable.
In contrast, our theory predicts that the joint probabilities of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, and
F
and
G
, are definable if the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
can be measure in a single trial and if
F
and
G
are proxy representations of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
.
For example, in the measurement of the position and momentum of charged particles, it is possible to estimate the functions
\mathcal{F}\,{=}\,{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathcal{G}\,{=}\,{\mathcal{P}}
of the arrangement of water droplets in a single trial.
Moreover,
{F}\,{=}\,{\bm{r}}
and
{G}\,{=}\,{\bm{p}}
become proxy representations of
\mathcal{F}\,{=}\,{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathcal{G}\,{=}\,{\mathcal{P}}
if we adjust the precision
\delta{F}
and
\delta{G}
such that the compatibility conditions between ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t
’ are satisfied (Eqs.(
185
)–(
186
)).
To this end, we have only to require

\displaystyle\exists i,\penalty\ \exists i^{\prime},\quad 1=\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{F}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)\rangle=\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{G}_{i^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}\right),t$'}\big)\rangle.

(194)

This correctly explains the experimental fact that the instantaneous values of position
{F}\,{=}\,{\bm{r}}
and momentum
{G}\,{=}\,{\bm{p}}
can be obtained simultaneously in a single trial when the measurement precision is sufficiently coarse compared to the uncertainty limit.

As another example, in the measurement of the
x
and
y
components of the spin angular momentum, measurements provide the instantaneous values of the following physical quantities:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar}{2}\Big({x}\big(\mbox{`$\mbox{detected}_{{\uparrow}_{x}}$'}\big)-{x}\big(\mbox{`$\mbox{detected}_{{\downarrow}_{x}}$'}\big)\Big)=\hbar\left({x}\big(\mbox{`$\mbox{detected}_{{\uparrow}_{x}}$'}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\right),

(195)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar}{2}\Big({x}\big(\mbox{`$\mbox{detected}_{{\uparrow}_{y}}$'}\big)-{x}\big(\mbox{`$\mbox{detected}_{{\downarrow}_{y}}$'}\big)\Big)=\hbar\left({x}\big(\mbox{`$\mbox{detected}_{{\uparrow}_{y}}$'}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\right),

(196)

where logical variables
\{{x}(\mbox{`o(${\uparrow}_{x}$),\,$t$'}),{x}(\mbox{`o(${\downarrow}_{x}$),\,$t$'})\}
and
\{{x}(\mbox{`o(${\uparrow}_{y}$),\,$t$'}),{x}(\mbox{`o(${\downarrow}_{y}$),\,$t$'})\}
represent the detection signals from two separate Stern-Gerlach experiments with different magnetic field directions (Eq.(
128
)).
Then, operators
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
satisfy
\big[\hat{\mathcal{F}},\hat{\mathcal{G}}\big]_{-}\,{=}\,0
, as can be confirmed by the first-order perturbation analysis of particle detectors.
This indicates that, according to conventional theory, the joint probability of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
should be definable.
In this case, however, the experiments are conducted separately, so the joint probability of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
is meaningless in practice, and as a result, the effects of measurement contextuality need to be carefully considered.
In our theory, on the other hand, when the instantaneous values of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
cannot be obtained in a single trial, their joint probability cannot be defined, eliminating the need for special commentary on the experimental facts of measurement contextuality.

5.3
Classical approximations and classical equations of motion

5.3.1
Classical approximations for instantaneous values of physical quantities

To formulate a classical approximation for instantaneous values of physical quantities, we approximate the instantaneous value using a single logical variable as

\displaystyle F^{(j)}(t)\cong F^{(j)}_{0}\,{x^{(j)}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{F}^{(j)}_{0}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\right),\quad\forall j

(197)

and adjust the precision
\delta{F}
of proposition ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{F}^{(j)}_{0}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ to satisfy the following condition:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{F}^{(j)}_{0}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\right)=1,\quad\forall j.

(198)

Since the compatibility conditions are always satisfied among propositions of this kind (§
B.1
), the classical approximation allows arithmetic operations between any physical quantities.
At the same time, however, taking the expectation value of Eq.(
197
), we obtain

\displaystyle F^{(j)}(t)=F^{(j)}_{0}=\langle F(t)\rangle,

(199)

meaning that the classical approximation requires the ensemble definition and the grid center to be continuously updated so that the expectation value must be equal to the instantaneous value.

For this reason, we denote the proposition ’The instantaneous value of
F
at time
t
is
F^{(j)}(t)
’ by the symbol ’
F^{(j)}(t)
’ and define the conditional expectation value conditioned by proposition ‘
F^{(j)}(t)
’ as

\displaystyle\Big\langle\,F(t)\,\Big|\!\Big|\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\Big\rangle\equiv\frac{\sum_{j^{\prime}}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}}F^{(j^{\prime})}(t)}{\sum_{j^{\prime}}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}}1}=F^{(j)}(t),\quad\forall j.

(200)

It then follows from Assumptions B1–B14 and the definition of conditional probabilities (Eq.(
49
)) that the density operator
\hat{\rho}(t|\text{'$F^{(j)}(t)$'})
in the classical approximation satisfies

\displaystyle\Big\langle\,F(t)\,\Big|\!\Big|\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\Big\rangle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{F}\,\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)\,\Big],

(201)

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big)\equiv\frac{\hat{z}\big(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{z}^{\dagger}\big(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)}{{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{z}(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{z}^{\dagger}(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'})\,\Big]}.

(202)

Here, the projection operator
\hat{z}\big(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)
on the right-hand side of Eq.(
202
) is defined by

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)=\sum^{\{\!\!\{{F^{(j)}(t)}\}\!\!\}}_{|\varphi\rangle}|\varphi\rangle\langle\varphi|,

(203)

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{F^{(j)}(t)}\}\!\!\}\equiv\bigg\{\,\forall|\varphi\rangle\,\bigg|\,F^{(j)}(t)-\frac{\delta{F}_{i-1}}{2}\,{\leqq}\,F(\varphi)\,{<}\,F^{(j)}(t)+\frac{\delta{F}_{i}}{2},\penalty\ \hat{F}|\varphi\rangle=F(\varphi)|\varphi\rangle,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \langle\varphi|\varphi^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}}\,\bigg\},

(204)

with the corresponding Heisenberg operator given by

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t),t^{\prime}$'}\big)=\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime}-t)\,\hat{z}\big(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)\,\hat{U}(t^{\prime}-t),\quad t^{\prime}\geqq t.

(205)

Using Eqs.(
202
)–(
205
), we can see that the density operator and the Heisenberg operator satisfy

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big)

\displaystyle={\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big){\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big),

(206)

\displaystyle\hat{F}(t)

\displaystyle=\hat{z}\big(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)\,\hat{F}(t)\,\hat{z}\big(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big).

(207)

These equations show that, in the classical approximation, we need to keep the precision
\delta{F}
of proposition representation low enough so that projection operator
{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{\text{'$F^{(j)}(t)$'}}\big)
does not affect the time evolution of
\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big)
and
\hat{F}(t)
.
It should also be noted that in the classical approximation, we have

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)=\mathrm{Tr}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big)\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big)\,\Big]=1,\quad\forall j,

(208)

where
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
is the stationary state of the entire system occupied at time
t
.

\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}
is the set of stationary states containing
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
.
Combining Eq.(
206
) and Eq.(
208
), we find that the characteristic operator in the Heisenberg picture satisfies

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle=\hat{x}\big(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)\,\hat{x}\big(\,\mbox{`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big)

(209)

\displaystyle=\hat{x}\big(\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}}),t$'$\wedge$`$F^{(j)}(t)$'}\big).

(210)

5.3.2
Complementarity principle

To derive the classical equations of motion, we need to specify the simultaneous values of position and momentum.
While this was not possible under the standard formulation, with the compatibility condition, it becomes possible by lowering the precision of proposition representation.
According to Eq.(
210
), the classical approximation requires that proposition ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}})
’ satisfy

‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}})
’

\displaystyle=\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}})$'$\wedge$`${\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)$'$\wedge$`${\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)$'}

(211)

\displaystyle=\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}\,{\cap}\,\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)}\}\!\!\}\,{\cap}\,\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)}\}\!\!\}})$'},

(212)

which can be achieved by setting the precision of position
\delta{\bm{r}}
and the precision of momentum
\delta{\bm{p}}
so that

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{(j)}_{\Omega}(t)}\}\!\!\}\subseteq\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)}\}\!\!\}\cap\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)}\}\!\!\}.

(213)

If we define the variance of a physical quantity
F
as

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Var}}_{F}(t)\equiv\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{F}-\Big\langle{F}(t)\Big|\!\Big|\mbox{`${\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)$'$\wedge$`${\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)$'}\Big\rangle\right)^{2}\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`${\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)$'$\wedge$`${\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big)\right],

(214)

Eq.(
213
) should hold if

\displaystyle\delta{r}_{\xi}\gg\sqrt{{\mathrm{Var}}_{r_{\xi}}(t)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\delta{p}_{\xi}\gg\sqrt{{\mathrm{Var}}_{p_{\xi}}(t)},\quad\xi\in\{x,y,z\}.

(215)

Meanwhile, the Robertson inequality
[
67
]
in quantum theory guarantees that

\displaystyle\sqrt{{\mathrm{Var}}_{F}(t)}\sqrt{{\mathrm{Var}}_{G}(t)}\geqq\frac{1}{2}\,\Bigg|{\mathrm{Tr}}\left[\,\left[\hat{F},\hat{G}\right]_{-}\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`${\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)$'$\wedge$`${\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big)\right]\Bigg|,

(216)

indicating that the classical approximation is valid when

\displaystyle\delta{r}_{\xi}\,\delta{p}_{\xi}\gg\frac{1}{2}\,\Bigg|\,{\mathrm{Tr}}\left[\,\left[\hat{r}_{\xi},\hat{p}_{\xi}\right]_{-}\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`${\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)$'$\wedge$`${\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big)\right]\Bigg|=\frac{\hbar}{2},\quad\xi\in\{x,y,z\}

(217)

Eq.(
217
) presents the precision limit of classical approximation in individual trials, an explicit expression of the complementarity principle
[
14
]
.
Note, however, that we have not considered the possibility of squeezing
[
16
, Chap.21]
here.

5.3.3
Classical equations of motion

Next, we consider cases where instantaneous values of canonical variables and canonical momenta can be defined at closely spaced time intervals based on the classical approximation.
The density operator
\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`${\bm{r}}^{(j)}(t)$'$\wedge$`${\bm{p}}^{(j)}(t)$'}\,\big)
in the classical approximation is discontinuously updated by Eq.(
202
) at the moment new measurement values are obtained, and it changes continuously according to the quantum equation of motion between measurements.
However, the projection operator used to update the ensemble is non-sharp, as can be seen from Eqs.(
206
)–(
207
), so the time evolution of the density operator is not affected by the ensemble update operation.
Consequently, the density operator in the classical approximation changes according to the quantum equation of motion at all times, and according to Ehrenfest’s theorem
[
14
, Chap.6]
[
68
]
, the following classical equations of motion hold:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}q^{(j)}_{l}(t)=+\Bigg\langle\frac{\partial\hat{H}}{\partial\hat{p}_{l}}\,\Bigg|\!\Bigg|\,\bigwedge^{L}_{l=1}\mbox{`${q}^{(j)}(t)$'$\wedge$`${p}^{(j)}(t)$'}\Bigg\rangle,\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,l\,{\leqq}\,L,\quad\forall j,
