(218)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}p^{(j)}_{l}(t)=-\Bigg\langle\frac{\partial\hat{H}}{\partial\hat{q}_{l}}\,\Bigg|\!\Bigg|\,\bigwedge^{L}_{l=1}\mbox{`${q}^{(j)}(t)$'$\wedge$`${p}^{(j)}(t)$'}\Bigg\rangle,\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,l\,{\leqq}\,L,\quad\forall j.

(219)

Writing these equations explicitly for the position
{\bm{r}}_{k}(t)
and momentum
{\bm{p}}_{k}(t)
of charged particles (
1\,{\leqq}\,k\,{\leqq}\,K
) gives the classical equations of motion for charged particles:

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}{\bm{r}}^{(j)}_{k}(t),\quad\forall j,

(220)

\displaystyle m_{k}\frac{d}{dt}{\bm{v}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle={\bm{f}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{Lorentz},k}(t)+{\bm{f}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{spin},k}(t),\quad\forall j.

(221)

Here, we defined the velocity of the
k
-th charged particle as

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{m_{k}}\left(\hat{\bm{p}}_{k}-e_{k}{\hat{\bm{A}}_{\mathrm{T}}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})\right),

(222)

\displaystyle{\bm{v}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{m_{k}}\left({\bm{p}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)-e_{k}{{\bm{A}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}}({\bm{r}}^{(j)}_{k}(t),t)\right),

(223)

and defined the Lorentz force and spin force acting on the
k
-th charged particle by the following equations:

\displaystyle{\bm{f}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{Lorentz},k}(t)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Bigg[\,{e_{k}}\Bigg({\hat{\bm{E}}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})-\sum^{K}_{k^{\prime}=1}\frac{e_{k^{\prime}}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}-\hat{\bm{r}}_{k^{\prime}}}{\left\|\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}-\hat{\bm{r}}_{k^{\prime}}\right\|^{3}}+\hat{\bm{v}}_{k}\cdot{\hat{\bm{B}}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})\Bigg)\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{history}^{(j)}_{t}$'}\,\bigg)\,\Bigg],

(224)

\displaystyle{\bm{f}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{spin},k}(t)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Bigg[\,\Bigg(\frac{g_{k}|e_{k}|}{2m_{k}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}}\left({\hat{\bm{s}}}_{k}\cdot\hat{\bm{B}}(\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})\right)\Bigg)\,\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{history}^{(j)}_{t}$'}\,\big)\,\Bigg].

(225)

If we set the time of the first measurement (i.e., the time when water droplets first form in the cloud chamber) as
t\,{=}\,0
, then for
t\,{>}\,0
, the classical equations of motion hold due to continuous measurements (i.e., generation of localized stationary states), so there is no “spooky action at a distance.”
On the other hand, for
t\,{<}\,0
, charged particles may be in an entangled state, and at
t\,{=}\,0
, deterministic relation may be formed between the positions and momenta of different charged particles (Supplementary Material B).
This change at
t=0
is abrupt in the sense that predictions based on the continuity of physical quantities are not effective.
Still, there is no “spooky action at a distance” because there are no comparable measurement values for
t\,{\leqq}\,0
.
It is worth noting that the existence of deterministic relations between instantaneous values of position or momentum in individual trials is an experimental fact confirmed by Compton scattering experiments
[
64
,
69
,
70
]
.

According to Ehrenfest’s theorem, the classical equations of motion for the radiation field take the following form

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}{\bm{A}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle=-{\bm{E}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}},t),

(226)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}{\bm{B}}^{(j)}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle=-{\bm{\nabla}}\times{\bm{E}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}},t),

(227)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}{\bm{E}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{2}}{\bm{\nabla}}\times{\bm{B}}^{(j)}({\bm{x}},t)-\frac{1}{c^{2}}{\bm{J}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}},t),

(228)

where we defined the instantaneous values of magnetic flux density
{\bm{B}}
and the transverse component of current density
{\bm{J}}_{\mathrm{T}}
by

\displaystyle{\bm{B}}^{(j)}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle\equiv{\bm{\nabla}}\times{\bm{A}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}},t),

(229)

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle\equiv\iiint{\bm{\delta}}^{{\mathrm{T}}}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime})\cdot{\bm{J}}^{(j)}({\bm{x}}^{\prime},t)d{\bm{x}}^{\prime}.

(230)

Also, the instantaneous values of current density
{\bm{J}}
and charge density
{\sigma}
are defined as

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}^{(j)}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathrm{Tr}}\Bigg[\left(\sum^{K}_{k=1}e_{k}\frac{\hat{\bm{v}}_{k}\delta({\bm{x}}-\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})+\delta({\bm{x}}-\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})\hat{\bm{v}}_{k}}{2}\right)\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{history}^{(j)}_{t}$'}\,\big)\Bigg],

(231)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(j)}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathrm{Tr}}\Bigg[\left(\sum^{K}_{k=1}e_{k}\delta({\bm{x}}-\hat{\bm{r}}_{k})\right)\hat{\rho}\big(\,t\,\big|\,\mbox{`$\mathrm{history}^{(j)}_{t}$'}\,\big)\Bigg].

(232)

Combining these equations with the definition of the longitudinal component of the electric field
{\bm{E}}_{\mathrm{L}}
and the electric field
{\bm{E}}
, namely

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}\cdot{\bm{E}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{L}}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\sigma^{(j)}({\bm{x}},t),\quad{\bm{\nabla}}\times{\bm{E}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{L}}({\bm{x}},t)=0,

(233)

\displaystyle{\bm{E}}^{(j)}({\bm{x}},t)

\displaystyle\equiv{\bm{E}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{T}}({\bm{x}},t)+{\bm{E}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{L}}({\bm{x}},t),

(234)

we obtain the Maxwell equations of classical electromagnetism.
It is worth noting that, according to the above formulation, the classical equation of motion holds for individual trials before statistical averaging.
Note also that when describing the double-slit experiment, the discreteness of individual events (i.e. the particle nature of light) is explained by the discrete response of the photodetector, and interference and diffraction (i.e. the wave nature of light) are explained by solving the field equations
[
15
]
.

5.3.4
Quantum fluctuations

When a classical approximation
F^{(j)}(t)
exists for the instantaneous value of
F
, the instantaneous value of the quantum fluctuation of
F
can be defined as

\displaystyle\varDelta{F}^{(j)}(t)\equiv F^{(j)}(t)-\langle F(t)\rangle,\quad\forall j.

(235)

Here,
\langle F(t)\rangle
is the expectation value of
F
for the initial ensemble, while
F^{(j)}(t)
is the instantaneous value as well as the expectation value of
F
for the updated ensemble (Eq.(
200
)).
This highlights the importance of distinguishing between the two different statistical ensemble.
The power fluctuation can then be expressed as

\displaystyle\big\langle{F}(t)^{2}\big\rangle=\big|\langle{F}(t)\rangle\big|^{2}+\langle\varDelta{F}(t)^{2}\rangle,

(236)

which has traditionally been interpreted as the wave and particle components of light according to light quantum hypothesis.
On the other hand, in our theory, the terms on the right-hand side merely represent the contributions from the expected value and the deviation from it.

5.4
Bell’s theorem and CHSH inequality

5.4.1
Deterministic relations between spins of subsystems

We now focus on two spin-1/2 systems in an entangled state to explain the relationship between our theory and well-known no-go theorems.
First, to summarize our theory’s results, we denote the stationary state of the
k
-th subsystem (
k\,{\in}\,\{1,2\}
) as
|{\bm{u}}_{k},s_{k}\rangle^{\!k}
, with
{\bm{u}}_{k}
as the unit vector of the measurement direction and
s_{k}\,{\in}\,\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}
as the spin orientation.
For
t\,{>}\,0
, when interactions can be ignored, we can define logical variables for the subsystems as

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{k},s_{k}}\}^{{k}}}}),t$'}\Big)=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{$|{\bm{u}}_{k},s_{k}\rangle^{\!k}$ is occupied at time $t$},\\
0,&\mbox{$|{\bm{u}}_{k},s_{k}\rangle^{\!k}$ is not occupied at time $t$}\end{cases},\penalty\ \penalty\ k\in\{1,2\},

(237)

and the characteristic and partial characteristic operators take the following form:

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{k},s_{k}}\}^{{k}}}})$'}\big)={\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{k},s_{k}}\}^{{k}}}})$'}\big)=|{\bm{u}}_{k},s_{k}\rangle^{\!k}{}^{k}\!\langle{\bm{u}}_{k},s_{k}|,\quad k\in\{1,2\}.

(238)

It follows that compatibility conditions hold between the following sets of propositions:

\displaystyle\big\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{1},\uparrow}\}^{{1}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{1},\downarrow}\}^{{1}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{2},\uparrow}\}^{{2}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{2},\downarrow}\}^{{2}}}})$'}\big\},\quad\forall{\bm{u}}_{1},\quad\forall{\bm{u}}_{2}.

(239)

Particularly, if the system is known to be in a singlet state, namely

\displaystyle|0\rangle^{\!\mathrm{S}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\,|{\bm{u}},\uparrow\rangle^{\!1}\otimes|{\bm{u}},\downarrow\rangle^{\!2}-|{\bm{u}},\downarrow\rangle^{\!1}\otimes|{\bm{u}},\uparrow\rangle^{\!2}\,\Big),\quad{\bm{u}}\cdot{\bm{u}}=1,\quad{\bm{u}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},

(240)

then due to the general properties of entangled subsystems (§
5.1.3
), the following deterministic relations exist when the measurement direction
{\bm{u}}
is common:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}},\uparrow}\}^{{2}}}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}},\downarrow}\}^{{1}}}}),t$'}\big),

(241)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}},\downarrow}\}^{{2}}}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}},\uparrow}\}^{{1}}}}),t$'}\big).

(242)

The formula for the joint probability (§
5.1.3
) is the same as in quantum theory
[
8
, Eq.(7.39)]
:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{1},s_{1}}\}^{{1}}}}),t$'}\,{\wedge}\,\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{2},s_{2}}\}^{{2}}}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{1},s_{1}}\}^{{1}}}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{2},s_{2}}\}^{{2}}}}),t$'}\big)\big\rangle

(243)

\displaystyle={}^{\mathrm{S}}\!\langle\,0\,|\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{1},s_{1}}\}^{{1}}}})$'}\big)\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{u}}_{2},s_{2}}\}^{{2}}}})$'}\big)\,|0\rangle^{\!\mathrm{S}}

(244)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-{\bm{u}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{u}}_{2}}{4}.

(245)

5.4.2
Relation to Bell’s Inequality

As is well known, Bell’s paper on hidden variable theories addressed a model of local physical reality where the joint probability
P({\bm{a}},{\bm{b}})
is determined by
[
3
, Eq.(2)]

\displaystyle P({\bm{a}},{\bm{b}})\equiv\int A({\bm{a}},\lambda)\,B({\bm{b}},\lambda)\,\rho_{\lambda}d\lambda,\penalty\ \penalty\ \int\rho_{\lambda}d\lambda=1,\penalty\ \penalty\ \begin{cases}A({\bm{a}},\lambda)\in\{-1,+1\},\\
B({\bm{b}},\lambda)\in\{-1,+1\}.\end{cases}

(246)

Here,
\lambda
represents hidden variables,
\rho_{\lambda}
is the probability density function of hidden variables, and
A({\bm{a}},\lambda)
and
B({\bm{b}},\lambda)
represent instantaneous spin values when measurement directions are
{\bm{a}}
and
{\bm{b}}
, and the hidden variable is
\lambda
.
Bell’s inequality
[
3
, Eq.(15)]
, namely

\displaystyle 1+P({\bm{b}},{\bm{c}})\geqq\left|P({\bm{a}},{\bm{b}})-P({\bm{a}},{\bm{c}})\right|,

(247)

was derived from Eq.(
246
), deliberately ignoring standard formulas in quantum theory, to elucidate the properties of hidden variables.
In contrast, as our theory’s joint probability equations are the same as quantum theory’s, our theory is not a hidden variable theory of the type Bell assumed.
Therefore, the violation of Bell’s inequality is irrelevant to the validity of our theory.

5.4.3
Relation to CHSH inequality

The derivation of the CHSH inequality
[
5
]
pointed out that assuming definite spin values in individual measurements leads to contradictions.
If we define spin variables that take either
+1
or
-1
for measurement directions
{\bm{a}}
,
{\bm{b}}
,
{\bm{c}}
,
{\bm{d}}
as

\displaystyle A^{(j)}({\bm{a}})=2{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{a}},\uparrow}\}^{{{1}}}}})$'}\big)-1,\quad C^{(j)}({\bm{c}})=2{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{c}},\uparrow}\}^{{{1}}}}})$'}\big)-1,

(248)

\displaystyle B^{(j)}({\bm{b}})=2{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{b}},\uparrow}\}^{{{2}}}}})$'}\big)-1,\quad D^{(j)}({\bm{d}})=2{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{d}},\uparrow}\}^{{{2}}}}})$'}\big)-1,

(249)

and forget about the constraints due to the compatibility condition, we can show that
[
13
, §2.6]

\displaystyle A^{(j)}({\bm{a}})B^{(j)}({\bm{b}})+B^{(j)}({\bm{b}})C^{(j)}({\bm{c}})+C^{(j)}({\bm{c}})D^{(j)}({\bm{d}})-D^{(j)}({\bm{d}})A^{(j)}({\bm{a}})=\pm 2,\penalty\ \forall{j}.

(250)

This led to the CHSH inequality
[
5
]
:

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle A({\bm{a}})B({\bm{b}})\big\rangle+\big\langle B({\bm{b}})C({\bm{c}})\big\rangle+\big\langle C({\bm{c}})D({\bm{d}})\big\rangle-\big\langle D({\bm{d}})A({\bm{a}})\big\rangle\Big|\leqq 2

(251)

On the other hand, when

\displaystyle{\bm{a}}={\bm{e}}_{z},\quad{\bm{b}}=-\frac{{\bm{e}}_{z}+{\bm{e}}_{x}}{\sqrt{2}},\quad{\bm{c}}={\bm{e}}_{x},\quad{\bm{d}}=\frac{{\bm{e}}_{z}-{\bm{e}}_{x}}{\sqrt{2}},

(252)

the joint probability formula of quantum theory (Eq.(
245
)) gives

\displaystyle\big\langle A({\bm{a}})B({\bm{b}})\big\rangle+\big\langle B({\bm{b}})C({\bm{c}})\big\rangle+\big\langle C({\bm{c}})D({\bm{d}})\big\rangle-\big\langle D({\bm{d}})A({\bm{a}})\big\rangle=2\sqrt{2},

(253)

contradicting Eq.(
251
).

In standard formulation of quantum theory, this contradiction was explained as arising from assuming integer spin values (
+1
or
-1
).
However, our theory suggests the true cause of contradiction is considering arithmetic operations that do not satisfy compatibility conditions.
Indeed, using the expression of partial characteristic operator in Eq.(
238
), we find compatibility conditions hold for combinations

\displaystyle\big\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{a}},\uparrow}\}^{{{1}}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{b}},\uparrow}\}^{{{2}}}}})$'}\big\},\quad\big\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{b}},\uparrow}\}^{{{2}}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{c}},\uparrow}\}^{{{1}}}}})$'}\big\},

(254)

\displaystyle\big\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{c}},\uparrow}\}^{{{1}}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{d}},\uparrow}\}^{{{2}}}}})$'}\big\},\quad\big\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{d}},\uparrow}\}^{{{2}}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{a}},\uparrow}\}^{{{1}}}}})$'}\big\},

(255)

but not for

\displaystyle\big\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{a}},\uparrow}\}^{{{1}}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{c}},\uparrow}\}^{{{1}}}}})$'}\big\},\quad\big\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{b}},\uparrow}\}^{{{2}}}}})$'},\penalty\ \mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{{\bm{d}},\uparrow}\}^{{{2}}}}})$'}\big\}.

(256)

Therefore, while the multiplication of spin variables, namely

\displaystyle A({\bm{a}})B({\bm{b}}),\quad B({\bm{b}})C({\bm{c}}),\quad C({\bm{c}})D({\bm{d}}),\quad D({\bm{d}})A({\bm{a}}),

(257)

can be defined without issue, the following addition violates compatibility conditions and cannot be defined:

\displaystyle A({\bm{a}})B({\bm{b}})+B({\bm{b}})C({\bm{c}})+C({\bm{c}})D({\bm{d}})+D({\bm{d}})A({\bm{a}}).

(258)

In a nutshell, the CHSH inequality does not hold in our theory because the compatibility condition restricts arithmetic operations between instantaneous values.
Similarly, assuming compatibility conditions resolves other contradictions related to measurement contextuality, though we omit detailed analysis of individual cases for brevity.

5.5
Issues with the Light Quantum Hypothesis

Modern quantum optics theory explains the statistical properties of radiation fields solely through the quantization of atomic systems and radiation fields.
From a modern perspective, the most compelling evidence for the existence of photons is the observation of discontinuous individual events and their deterministic relations (See supplementary material).
Considering this, the essence of the light quantum hypothesis can be expressed in the following deterministic relation
[
7
]
:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`absorbed,\,$t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`ionized,\,$t$'}\big),\quad\forall j,

(259)

where ‘ionized,
t
’ represents a proposition of the atomic system meaning ‘the atom is photoionized at time
t
’, and ‘absorbed,
t
’ represents a proposition of the radiation field meaning ‘a photon is absorbed by the atomic system at time
t
’.
As demonstrated in Supplementary Material B, the proposition ‘ionized,
t
’ can accurately reproduce the standard formula for light detection probability when defined as:

\displaystyle\mbox{`ionized,\,$t$'}=\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}}),0$'},\quad t\,{>}\,0.

(260)

Here,
\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}
and
\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}
are the sets of all bound states and scattering states of the atomic system, respectively.
However, for the proposition ‘absorbed,
t
’, no representation of a partial characteristic operator consistent with quantum optical theory exists, highlighting a limitation of the light quantum hypothesis from our perspective.

To illustrate this more concretely, let us first consider the case where the radiation field is initially in the
n\,{=}\,1
Fock state
|1\rangle
.
In this case, we define the proposition ‘absorbed,
t
’ as

\displaystyle\mbox{`absorbed,\,$t$'}\equiv\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\mbox{vac}}\}}}),t$'${\wedge}$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{1}\}}}),0$'},\quad t\,{>}\,0,

(261)

We can then show that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`absorbed,\,$t$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]={\mathrm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`ionized,\,$t$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big],

(262)

using the relations

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`absorbed,\,$t$'}\big)

\displaystyle=|1\rangle\langle 1|\,\hat{U}(t)\,|\mbox{vac}\rangle\langle\mbox{vac}|\,\hat{U}(t)\,|1\rangle\langle 1|,

(263)

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})

\displaystyle=|{\mathrm{1s}}\rangle\langle{\mathrm{1s}}|\otimes|1\rangle\langle 1|.

(264)

Here,
|\mbox{vac}\rangle
denotes the vacuum state of the radiation field,
|{\mathrm{1s}}\rangle
the bound state of the atomic system, and
\hat{U}(t)
the time evolution operator (Supplementary Material B).
This result (Eq.(
262
)) aligns with the predictions of the light quantum hypothesis (Eq.(
259
)).
Then, by first-order perturbation calculation similar to that shown in Supplementary Material B, we can show that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`absorbed,\,$t$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]={\mathrm{Tr}}_{\bm{r}}\big[\,\langle\psi(t)|\mbox{vac}\rangle\langle\mbox{vac}|\psi(t)\rangle\,\big],

(265)

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle\equiv\hat{U}(t)\big|{\mathrm{1s}}\big\rangle|1\rangle\cong c_{\mathrm{1s}}(t)\big|{\mathrm{1s}}\big\rangle\big|1\big\rangle+\iiint c_{{\bm{p}}}(t)\big|{\bm{p}}\big\rangle|\mbox{vac}\rangle\,d{\bm{p}},

(266)

where
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\bm{r}}
is the trace operation for the atomic system,
|\psi(t)\rangle
is the state vector of the atomic system, and
|{\bm{p}}\rangle
is the scattering state of the atomic system.
From the right-hand side of Eq.(
266
), we can see that the atomic system and radiation field are entangled at
t\,{>}\,0
when the radiation field is initially in the
n\,{=}\,1
Fock state.
Additionally, due to the general properties of entangled subsystems (§
5.1.3
), there exist a deterministic relation between the stationary states of the atomic system and the radiation field, which is the the light quantum hypothesis of Eq.(
259
).

On the other hand, when the radiation field is initially in a coherent state
|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle
, the state vector in Eq.(
266
) will have the following form:

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle\equiv\hat{U}(t)\big|{\mathrm{1s}}\big\rangle|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle\cong\left(c_{\mathrm{1s}}(t)\big|{\mathrm{1s}}\big\rangle+\iiint c_{{\bm{p}}}(t)\big|{\bm{p}}\big\rangle\,d{\bm{p}}\right)|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle.

(267)

In this case, the atomic system and radiation field are not entangled, and substituting Eq.(
267
) into equation Eq.(
265
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`absorbed,\,$t$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big]

\displaystyle=\big|\langle\mbox{vac}|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle\big|^{2}\,\iiint\big|c_{{\bm{p}}}(t)\big|^{2}\,d{\bm{p}}

(268)

\displaystyle=\big|\langle\mbox{vac}|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle\big|^{2}\,{\mathrm{Tr}}\big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`ionized,\,$t$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\big],

(269)

which contradicts Eq.(
262
) as required by the light quantum hypothesis of Eq.(
259
).
Therefore, the definition of proposition ’absorbed,
t
’ by Eq.(
261
) does not hold generally, and in this sense, the light quantum hypothesis is limited as a quantitative model of reality.

6
Discussion

Given the pivotal role of stationary states in our theory, it is imperative to elucidate the unresolved issues pertaining to their definition.
We begin by expounding on the rationale behind the definition of stationary states as delineated in Assumptions B1–B14.

Rationale for the definition of stationary states

In this paper, we have defined stationary states using the non-interacting Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}\equiv{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}^{\dagger}\,\left(\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}\right){\hat{\mathsf{U}}},

(270)

where
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}
represent the Hamiltonians of the atomic system and radiation field, respectively, and
\hat{\mathsf{U}}
denotes a time-independent unitary transformation.
Our decision to eschew the total Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{{\mathsf{W}}}\equiv{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}^{\dagger}\,\left(\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{I}}\right){\hat{\mathsf{W}}}

(271)

in defining stationary states is predicated on the experimental findings of Dehmelt et al.
[
20
]
, which demonstrated that the frequency of quantum jumps approximates the inverse of the radiative lifetime.
This experimental observation aligns with the definition of stationary states as eigenstates of
\hat{H}^{{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}
, but is incongruent with a definition based on the eigenstates of
\hat{H}^{{\mathsf{W}}}
.
The inclusion of the radiation field Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}
in Eq.(
270
) is motivated by the experiments of Haroche et al.
[
23
]
, which observed quantum jumps between Fock states of radiation fields.
Furthermore, it has become apparent that representing light with well-defined phase (e.g., weak laser light) through the occupation of a finite set of Fock states is problematic.
Consequently, the definition of stationary states must encompass quantum states with small phase uncertainty, such as coherent states.
This necessitates the incorporation of the time-independent unitary transformation
{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
in Eq.(
270
).

Ambiguity in the definition of unitary transformation

The introduction of the time-independent unitary transformation
{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
raises new questions regarding the permissible range of unitary transformations.
In fact, for the purpose of including coherent states, it suffices to consider the vacuum displacement operator:

\displaystyle{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}=\prod_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}e^{\tilde{\alpha}^{*}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}\hat{a}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}-\tilde{\alpha}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}},\quad\tilde{\alpha}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{C}},

(272)

where
\hat{a}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}
and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{{\bm{k}}\lambda}
are the ladder operators for the
{\bm{k}}\lambda
-mode of radiation fields.
However, the choice of
{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
depends on the choice of
{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}
in Eq.(
271
).
The selection of
{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}
is intricately linked to subtle issues, including the choice between minimal coupling and multipolar Hamiltonians
[
16
, §14.1.3]
, methods for incorporating radiative corrections
[
33
,
35
,
71
]
, and considerations related to gauge degrees of freedom.
Consequently, the extent to which the definition of
{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
should be expanded remains ambiguous.
Conversely, limiting the scope of
{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
effectively constrains the permissible models of reality, rendering the choice of
{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
physically significant.
Therefore, in this paper, we have refrained from specifying the allowable ranges of
{\hat{\mathsf{U}}}
and
{\hat{\mathsf{W}}}
, leaving their forms undetermined.

Challenges in Relativistic Extension

Our theory is further constrained by its non-relativistic nature.
The concept of stationary states lacks relativistic invariance, and relativistic theories face the problem of gauge ghosts.
Consequently, any attempt to extend our approach to a relativistic framework would likely necessitate fundamental modifications.
More specifically, our theory involves two types of time evolution: one based on the non-interacting Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{{\mathsf{U}}}_{0}
and another on the total Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{{\mathsf{W}}}
.
However, at present, we lack a clear path to reformulate the former time evolution in a relativistic manner.
In this sense, our theory can be viewed as a phenomenological description focused solely on representing individual events observed in quantum optics experiments.
It should be noted, however, that non-relativistic theories are not without merit, as evidenced by the fact that quantum optical theories can effectively account for the effects of propagation delay
[
36
]
.
Furthermore, even in the non-relativistic case, the standard formulation fails to represent the world of reality (i.e., integer functions obtained in individual trials).
Therefore, at this juncture, a phenomenological approach is undeniably necessary to represent empirical laws of reality.

Insufficient information on the laws of reality

A more fundamental issue is the current paucity of knowledge regarding the laws of reality.
As explained in Section
1
, the laws of reality envisioned by our theory are not differential equations for continuous functions, but rather constraints on propositions at different times or for different subsystems.
Examples of laws of reality include quantum jumps from bound states to scattering states in photoexcited atoms, the validity of classical equations of motion when representing charged particle motion at low precision, and the existence of deterministic relations in EPR-inspired experiments.
The light quantum hypothesis can also be considered a law of reality, albeit one that is valid only for specific initial conditions.
Whether there are other empirical laws of reality remains a matter for debate, and indeed, the utility of knowledge about these laws is yet to be fully understood.
Moreover, to represent the laws of reality, it is necessary to specify the stationary state of the entire system at each instant using a subspace
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}
, with the condition
{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}
not being uniquely determined.
Nevertheless, our theory suggests that it is possible to assess the validity of representations using compatibility conditions.
Therefore, we argue that a phenomenological hidden variable theory remains effective in representing experimental data of integer functions, even if it deviates from the standard formulation.

7
Conclusions

This paper focuses on propositions with probabilities predictable by quantum theory, formulating a hidden variable theory that describes the occupation of stationary states using Boolean logical variables.
A key feature of this theory is a novel selection rule for propositions, derived from the need to harmonize Boolean logic with quantum theory.
This selection rule extends the definition of joint probability, allowing deterministic behavior to be derived from the stochastic laws of quantum theory.
Simultaneously, it avoids the constraints of major no-go theorems by restricting arithmetic operations between physical quantities.
In this theory, the most fundamental hidden variables are logical variables specifying the occupied stationary state
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
at each moment.
However, such a sharp representation offers no special advantage due to the stochastic time variation of
|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle

On the other hand, in a non-sharp representation based on a set of stationary states
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}}\}\!\!\}
, logical variables may exhibit deterministic behavior, allowing deterministic relations to be established between logical variables of subsystems.
Significantly, these deterministic relations may represent laws of reality that the standard formulation of quantum theory cannot capture.
In describing the laws of reality, the crucial aspect is not the time variation of logical variables, but rather the interrelationships of conditions
{\mathrm{C}}_{\Omega}
representing the status of subsystems.
Using propositions whose probabilities are predictable by quantum theory, it becomes possible to describe integer functions and physical quantities whose expectation values can be predicted by quantum theory.
In our theory, the existence of simultaneous values of physical quantities is determined not by the commutativity of operators, but by the compatibility condition.
We also defined proxy representations for the instantaneous values of physical quantities that cannot be measured without additional measuring instruments, demonstrating how subtle experimental facts about measurement contextuality are accounted for.
Based on these results, we argue that a hidden variable theory for non-relativistic QED is not a theory of
point particles such as photons, but rather a theory of stationary states and individual events.
However, the definition of stationary states requires further investigation, limiting our theory to a phenomenological approach that aims solely to represent experimental data of integer functions.

Appendix A
Issues with representing individual trials using density operators

In this section, we show that assuming binary logical variables and their expectation values can be represented by density operators leads to inconsistencies, even without assuming the differentiability of density operators.
To this end, consider the following set of assumptions:

(i)

The occupation of a stationary state
|\phi\rangle
can be represented by a binary logical variable:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{$\phi=\Omega^{(j)}(t)$}\\
0,&\mbox{$\phi\neq\Omega^{(j)}(t)$}\end{cases},\quad 1\leqq j\leqq N.

(273)

(ii)

The expectation value of the logical variable is defined in a statistical sense, so that

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)\big\rangle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big).

(274)

(iii)

The expectation value of the logical variable can be expressed using the density operator
\hat{\rho}(t)
as follows:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)\big\rangle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(t)\,\Big].

(275)

(iv)

The operator
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'})
is a projection operator onto the stationary state
|\phi\rangle
, namely

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)\equiv|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|.

(276)

(v)

The logical variable can be represented using the density operator
\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)
for individual trials as

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}}),t$'}\big)={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi}\}}})$'}\big)\,\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)\,\Big].

(277)

Here,
j
is the trial number,
N
is the total number of trials, and
\phi
represents a complete set of quantum numbers of the stationary state.

|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle
is the stationary state occupied at time
t
in the
j
-th trial and satisfies the normalization condition
\big|\langle\Omega^{(j)}(t)|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle\big|\,{=}\,1
.
From assumptions (ii), (iii), (iv), and (v), for any
|\phi\rangle
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi\,\Big|\,\hat{\rho}(t)\,\Big|\phi\right\rangle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\langle\phi\,\Bigg|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)\,\Bigg|\phi\right\rangle.

(278)

If the order of the inner product and the limit operations is interchangeable, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t).

(279)

On the other hand, if we assume that the density operator
\hat{\rho}(t)
is pure, i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=|\psi(t)\rangle\langle\psi(t)|,\quad\langle\psi(t)|\psi(t)\rangle=1,

(280)

then by substituting

\displaystyle\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)=|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle\langle\Omega^{(j)}(t)|

(281)

into Eq.(
279
) and taking
\langle\psi(t)|\ldots|\psi(t)\rangle
on both sides, we get

\displaystyle 1=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left|\langle\psi(t)|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle\right|^{2}.

(282)

Since the term in the sum on the right-hand side has an absolute value of 1 or less by definition, Eq.(
281
) implies

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi(t)|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle\right|^{2}=1,\quad\forall j.

(283)

Therefore, if we simultaneously require assumptions (i)–(v), for a pure system, we obtain

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle=\exp({\mathrm{i}}\theta^{(j)})\,|\Omega^{(j)}(t)\rangle,\quad\theta^{(j)}\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad\forall j.

(284)

This conclusion contradicts the well-known fact that the state vector can be a superposition of stationary states, indicating that there must be an unreasonable assumption among (i)-(v).

Note that there are two completely different interpretations of this fact.
According to the first interpretation that denies assumption (i), the occupation of stationary states cannot be represented by logical variables.
This interpretation corresponds to the conventional explanation that regards Gleason’s theorem as a no-go theorem for hidden variable theories
[
8
]
.
On the other hand, the second interpretation that denies assumption (v), the occupation of stationary states can be described using logical variables if individual trials are not represented by density operators.
Interestingly, in the classical approximation (§
5.3
), individual trials can be described by density operators (
\check{\rho}^{(j)}(t)\,{=}\,\hat{\rho}(t)
) because assumption (ii) is removed by updating the ensemble according to measurement results.

Appendix B
Details of compatibility conditions

B.1
Propositions that are always true for specific ensembles

In this section, we show that compatibility conditions always hold between propositions that are true with 100% probability.
For this purpose, in the definition of sets of stationary states,

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}\equiv\left\{\,|\phi_{i}\rangle\,\Big|\penalty\ \hat{H}_{0}|\phi_{i}\rangle\,{=}\,E(\phi_{i})|\phi_{i}\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\phi_{i}|\phi^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi_{i},\phi^{\prime}_{i}},\penalty\ \mbox{$|\phi_{i}\rangle$ satisfy $\mathrm{C}_{i}$}\right\},\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I,

(285)

we consider the case where (i) different sets of stationary states are not necessarily orthogonalized (i.e., not necessarily
\langle\phi_{i}|\phi_{i^{\prime}}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\phi_{i},\phi_{i^{\prime}}}
when
|\phi_{i}\rangle\,{\in}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
and
|\phi_{i^{\prime}}\rangle\,{\in}\,\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}_{i^{\prime}}\}\!\!\}
), and (ii) all
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}\}\!\!\}
(
1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I
) are occupied with 100% probability at a given time
t_{i}
.

Then, due to assumption (ii), we have

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{i}$'}}\big)=1,\quad\forall{j},\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I.

(286)

Therefore, by the definition of expectation values (Assumption B9) and the laws of quantum theory (Assumptions B10, B11, B13), we obtain

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{i}$'}}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}})$'}}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t_{i}\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}})$'}}\big)\,\Big]

(287)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\}\!\!\}}\langle\,\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t_{i}\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\,\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,\rangle

(288)

\displaystyle=1,\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I.

(289)

Also, from the definition of logical variables (Assumption B5) and the definition of expectation values (Assumption B9), we see

\displaystyle 0\leqq\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{i}$'}}\big)\big\rangle\leqq 1,\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I

(290)

and from the Hermiticity of the density operator (Assumption B13), we see that

\displaystyle\langle\,\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t_{i}\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\,\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,\rangle\geqq 0

(291)

Therefore, when assumption (ii) holds, matrix elements of the density operator where
|\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle\,{\notin}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\}\!\!\}
or
|\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle\,{\notin}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\}\!\!\}
are always zero:

\displaystyle 0=\big\langle\,\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t_{i}\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\,\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\,\big\rangle=\big\langle\,\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t_{i}\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\,\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,\big\rangle.

(292)

Thus, when transforming the left-hand side of the compatibility condition (Eq.(
303
)) as

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{K}^{\dagger}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{K}^{\dagger}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}-1}}\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}-1}}\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\}\!\!\}})$'}\big)\,\Big]

(293)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\}\!\!\}}\sum_{|\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\}\!\!\}}\langle\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,\hat{K}^{\dagger}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}-1}}\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}-1}}\,|\,\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle\langle\,\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\,\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,\rangle,

(294)

based on Eq.(
292
), the sum on the right-hand side of Eq.(
294
) can be replaced with a sum over the entire Hilbert space:

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle}\sum_{|\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle}\langle\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,\hat{K}^{\dagger}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}-1}}\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}-1}}\,|\,\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\rangle\langle\,\phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I}}\,|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,|\,\phi_{\mathcal{I}}\,\rangle

(295)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}-1}}\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{K}^{\dagger}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}-1}}\,\Big].

(296)

By repeatedly using Eq.(
296
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{K}^{\dagger}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

(297)

and find that the left-hand side of Eq.(
291
) is independent of the order of partial characteristic operators.
Therefore, when the sets
\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}
(
1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I
) include all stationary states with finite occupation probability, compatibility conditions always hold between elementary propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{i}
’ (
1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I
).

B.2
Compatibility conditions for arbitrary number of propositions

In the main text, we derived compatibility conditions for two propositions ‘
\mathrm{A},\,t_{\mathrm{A}}
’ and ‘
\mathrm{B},\,t_{\mathrm{B}}
’.
Below, we derive compatibility conditions for
I
propositions more generally.
For this purpose, we make the following assumption:

Assumption A9
.

When propositions ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}
’ (
1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I
) are elementary propositions (defined at a single time), the probability of the compound proposition defined by

\displaystyle\mbox{`$\mathrm{A},\{t\}$'}\equiv\bigwedge^{I}_{i=1}\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}$'}\equiv\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1},t_{1}$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{A}}_{2},t_{2}$'$\wedge\ldots\wedge$`${\mathrm{A}}_{I},t_{\mathrm{I}}$'}

(298)

can be calculated by the following equation:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\big\rangle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\,\Big].

(299)

Here, we abbreviate the set of times
t_{1},\ldots,t_{I}
as
\{t\}
.

By substituting Eq.(
298
) into Eq.(
299
) and repeatedly applying the laws of Boolean operations (Assumption A5), we find that when all
t_{1},\ldots,t_{I}
are different from each other,
{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})
in Eq.(
299
) takes the following form:

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})=\hat{K}({\mathrm{A}}_{1},t_{1};\ldots;{\mathrm{A}}_{I},t_{I})\equiv\mathcal{T}\prod^{I}_{i=1}{\hat{z}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}$'}\right).

(300)

To clarify the correspondence with the Consistent Histories approach, we introduced the Chain operator
\hat{K}({\mathrm{A}}_{1},t_{1};\ldots;{\mathrm{A}}_{I},t_{I})
.
Substituting Eq.(
300
) into Eq.(
299
), we find the following relationship:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,$\{t\}$'})\big\rangle={\rm{Tr}}\left[\,\hat{K}({\mathrm{A}}_{1},t_{1};\ldots;{\mathrm{A}}_{I},t_{I})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\hat{K}({\mathrm{A}}_{1},t_{1};\ldots;{\mathrm{A}}_{I},t_{I})^{\dagger}\,\right].

(301)

For this joint probability to be uniquely defined at
t_{1}\,{=}\,\ldots\,{=}\,t_{I}\,{=}\,t
, the right-hand side of Eq.eeGen4 must be equal regardless of the time order of partial characteristic operators at
t_{1}\,{=}\,\ldots\,{=}\,t_{I}\,{=}\,t
.
Therefore, when assuming A9, we need to also assume the following:

Assumption A10
.

Let
\{i_{1},\,{\ldots}\,,i_{\mathcal{I}}\}
(
i_{1}\,{<}\,{\ldots}\,{<}\,i_{\mathcal{I}}
) be any combination of natural numbers extracted without duplication from the set of natural numbers
\{1,\,{\ldots}\,,I\}
.
Then, elementary propositions ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{i},t_{i}
’ (
1\,{\leqq}\,i\,{\leqq}\,I
) need to be chosen so that the product of partial characteristic operators

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\equiv{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i_{1}}$'})\cdots{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{i_{\mathcal{I}}}$'}),\quad 1\,{\leqq}\,\mathcal{I}\,{\leqq}\,I

(302)

satisfies the following relationship:

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}{}^{\dagger}\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\left({\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)^{\dagger}\,\Big],\quad 2\,{\leqq}\,\mathcal{I}\,{\leqq}\,I.

(303)

Here,
\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})
is a density operator, and
{\mathcal{P}}\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}
represents the operator obtained by rearranging the partial characteristic operators of
\hat{K}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathcal{I}}}
in any order.

Writing out Eq.(
303
) explicitly, we find that for
\mathcal{I}\,{=}\,2
, it becomes the same as Eq.(
44
) in the main text, and for
\mathcal{I}\,{=}\,3
, it takes the following form:

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

(304)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

(305)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

(306)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big]

(307)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`$\mathrm{A}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\Big].

(308)

Using the equation for conditional change of the density operator (Eq.(
49
)), these equations can be written in a form similar to the case of
\mathcal{I}\,{=}\,2
:

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1},t$'})\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1},t$'})\,\Big]

(309)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2},t$'})\,\Big]

(310)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2},t$'})\,\Big]

(311)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3},t$'})\,\Big]

(312)

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'}){\hat{z}^{\dagger}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{2}$'}){\hat{z}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{1}$'})\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{3},t$'})\,\Big].

(313)

Therefore, when considering the meaning of compatibility conditions, second-order compatibility conditions are particularly important.
Note that while the right-hand side of Eq.(
301
) is similar to the definition of correlation functions
[
16
, §11.12]
or many-body Green’s functions
[
61
, §3.7]
, it reduces to those equations only when partial characteristic operators (projection operators) can be replaced by creation and annihilation operators.

Supplementary material A: Model Details

A.1
The importance of exclusivity of occupation

This section supplements the role of exclusivity of occupation in the model of the world of reality shown in Section
4
.
First, we show that the assumption of exclusivity of occupation (Assumption B7) is necessary for Assumptions B1–B14 to be consistent.
It is sufficient to look at a simple example, so we consider the case where there are only two stationary states (
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}}\}\!\!\}\,{=}\,\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\}
and
\langle\phi_{1}|\phi_{2}\rangle\,{=}\,0
).
Then, by the definition of elementary propositions (Assumption B4), the definition of logical variables (Assumption B5), and the laws of Boolean logic (Assumption B6), we have

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'$\vee$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big),\quad{\forall}j

(314)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'}\big)+{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}})$'}\big)-{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big),\quad{\forall}j.

(315)

On the other hand, from the trace formula (Assumption B10), the Born rule (Assumption B11), and the laws of Boolean logic (Assumption B12), we find that

\displaystyle\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)\rangle=\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'}\big)\rangle+\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big)\rangle.

(316)

This relationship needs to hold for any initial ensemble ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{0},\,t_{0}
’, so by the definition of expectation values (Assumption B9), the following relationship needs to hold:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'}\big)+{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big),\quad{\forall}j.

(317)

Comparing Eq.(
315
) and Eq.(
317
), we can see that for Assumptions B4, B5, B6, B10, B12, and B13 to be consistent, the exclusivity of occupation (Assumption B7) must hold as

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big)=0\quad\mbox{for}\quad\phi_{1}\neq\phi_{2},

(318)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{1}}\}}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({{\{{\phi_{2}}\}}}),t$'}\big)\quad\mbox{for}\quad\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}.

(319)

Next, we see that the assumption of exclusivity of occupation (Assumption B7) is also necessary to rationally define elementary propositions.
To do this, we consider the case where the sets of stationary states
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}
representing groups
\{\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{C}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}\,|\,\mu\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{Z}}\}
of elementary propositions are mutually orthogonalized.
In this case, for the complement
\overline{\phantom{w}}
, intersection
\cap
, and union
\cup
of the sets of stationary states to have the properties:

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{\overline{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\,|\phi\rangle\,\Big|\,|\phi\rangle\,{\in}\,\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{E}}\}\!\!\},\,\mbox{$|\phi\rangle$ satisfies $\lnot\mathrm{C}^{\,\mu}$}\right\},\quad\mu\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{Z}}

(320)

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}\,{\cap}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\,|\phi\rangle\,\Big|\,|\phi\rangle\,{\in}\,\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{E}}\}\!\!\},\,\mbox{$|\phi\rangle$ satisfies $\mathrm{C}^{\mu}\,{\wedge}\,\mathrm{C}^{\mu^{\prime}}$}\right\},\quad\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(321)

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}\,{\cup}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\,|\phi\rangle\,\Big|\,|\phi\rangle\,{\in}\,\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{E}}\}\!\!\},\,\mbox{$|\phi\rangle$ satisfies $\mathrm{C}^{\mu}\,{\vee}\,\mathrm{C}^{\mu^{\prime}}$}\right\},\quad\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(322)

(where
\{\!\!\{{\mathrm{E}}\}\!\!\}
is the complete system of stationary states), the following relations need to hold between propositions defined at the same time:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{$\lnot$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{\overline{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

(323)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}\,{\wedge}\,{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}\,{\cap}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

(324)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'$\vee$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}\,{\vee}\,{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}}\}\!\!\}\,{\cup}\,\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big).

(325)

However, Eq.(
324
) reduces to the assumption of exclusivity of occupation (Assumption B7) when there are only two stationary states, so if we require Eq.(
320
)–(
322
) and (
323
)–(
325
), we need to assume the exclusivity of occupation (Assumption B7).
From the above, we have found that the exclusivity of occupation (Assumption B7) is necessary for the result of applying any Boolean operation to a group
\{\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}^{\mu}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{i}$'}\,|\,\mu\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{Z}}\}
of elementary propositions to be an elementary proposition (‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathrm{C}}_{i}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{i}
’).

A.2
Form of partial characteristic operators and deterministic relations

We show that when the partial characteristic operators of given propositions ‘A’ and ‘B’ are projection operators and are known to be equal for any density operator, there exists a deterministic relation between propositions ‘A’ and ‘B’.
First, we consider the case where the following relationship holds by assumption:

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})={\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}),\quad{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'})^{2}={\hat{z}}(\mbox{`A'}),\quad{\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'})^{2}={\hat{z}}(\mbox{`B'}).

(326)

In this case, since the compatibility condition holds between propositions ‘A’ and ‘B’, we can consider the conjunction ‘A,
t
’
\wedge
‘B,
t
’.
Also, by the trace formula (Assumption B10), operator transformation rule (Assumption B12), and equation of motion (Assumption B13), we have

\displaystyle\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'$\wedge$`B,\,$t$'})\rangle=\langle{x}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})\rangle=\langle{x}(\mbox{`B,\,$t$'})\rangle.

(327)

Therefore, according to the discussions in §
3.4
, the following deterministic relation holds between propositions ‘A,
t
’ and ‘B,
t
’:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`A,\,$t$'})={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`B,\,$t$'}),\quad\forall j.

(328)

A.3
Instantaneous value of total energy

In Dirac’s 1927 paper
[
38
, §7]
, it was pointed out that the energy conservation law for elementary processes does not hold in quantum theory due to the non-commutativity of the non-interacting Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{0}\,{=}\,\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}\,{+}\,\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}
and the interaction Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{I}}
.
This assertion is valid within the framework of the standard formulation and serves as an appropriate reason for rejecting the light quantum hypothesis.
In our theory, however, there is a possibility of simultaneous values existing for physical quantities with non-commuting operators, necessitating a reconsideration of this explanation.
In particular, the instantaneous value of the total energy stands as the sole exception to the definition of instantaneous values of physical quantities as described in the main text.
Therefore, we provide additional explanation below.

First, we define the instantaneous values of the total energy
{\mathcal{E}}^{(j)}(t)
and the energy of stationary states
E^{(j)}(t)
by the following equations:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{(j)}(t)=\sum_{{\mathcal{E}}}{\mathcal{E}}\,{x^{(j)}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{E}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\right),\quad\forall j,

(329)

\displaystyle E^{(j)}(t)=\sum_{E}E\,{x^{(j)}}\left(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{E}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\right),\quad\forall j,

(330)

where
\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{E}}\}\!\!\}
is a set of orthogonalized quantum states with total energy in the range
\mathcal{E}\,{\pm}\,{\delta{\mathcal{E}}}/{2}
6
6

6

Examples of quantum states with definite total energy include polariton states in infinite crystals
[
71
]
and dressed states of two-level atoms
[
33
]
.
, and
\{\!\!\{{E}\}\!\!\}
is a set of stationary states with energy in the range
E\,{\pm}\,\delta{E}/{2}
, namely

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{E}}\}\!\!\}\equiv\left\{\,|\Phi\rangle\,\Bigg|\,\hat{H}|\Phi\rangle=H|\Phi\rangle,\,\langle\Phi|\Phi^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}},\,{\mathcal{E}}-\frac{\delta{\mathcal{E}}}{2}\leqq H<{\mathcal{E}}+\frac{\delta{\mathcal{E}}}{2}\,\right\}

(331)

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{E}\}\!\!\}\equiv\left\{\,|\phi\rangle\,\Bigg|\,\hat{H}_{0}|\phi\rangle=H_{0}|\phi\rangle,\,\langle\phi|\phi^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}},\,E-\frac{\delta{E}}{2}\leqq H_{0}<E+\frac{\delta{E}}{2}\,\right\}.

(332)

Then, the following relation holds for any ensemble ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}
’:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big\langle\,{\mathcal{E}}(t)\,\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}{\mathrm{Tr}}\left[\hat{H}(t)\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\right]=\frac{1}{{\mathrm{i}}\hbar}{\mathrm{Tr}}\left[[\hat{H},\hat{H}]_{-}\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\right]=0.

(333)

On the other hand, in the classical approximation, if we set the precision of representation (
\delta{\mathcal{E}}
and
\delta{E}
) to satisfy the constraint of the complementarity principle (Eq.(
217
)),

\displaystyle\delta{\mathcal{E}}\,\delta{E}\gg\frac{1}{2}\,\Bigg|{\mathrm{Tr}}\left[\,\left[\hat{H},\hat{H}_{0}\right]_{-}\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0}$'},t)\right]\Bigg|=\frac{\hbar}{2}\,\Bigg|{\mathrm{Tr}}\left[\,\frac{d\hat{H}_{\mathrm{I}}(t)}{dt}\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\,\right]\Bigg|,

(334)

then the compatibility condition holds between propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{E}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{E}\}\!\!\}}),t
’.
Therefore, if we take time
t
before the start or after the end of the interaction, the right-hand side of Eq.(
334
) becomes zero, allowing us to make
\delta\mathcal{E}
and
\delta{E}
arbitrarily small.
Consequently, if we define the instantaneous value of the total energy
{\mathcal{E}}^{(j)}(t)
as a constant that coincides with the energy of the stationary state
{E}^{(j)}(t)
at times when the interaction energy can be ignored, then
{\mathcal{E}}^{(j)}(t)
takes a constant value for an initial ensemble ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}
’ where
{\mathcal{E}}^{(j)}(t)
has a single definite value.
Therefore, in our theory, while the light quantum hypothesis does not hold, it is possible to specify the instantaneous value of the total energy
{\mathcal{E}}^{(j)}(t)
with arbitrary precision.
Note that the instantaneous value of the total energy has this exceptional property because the total Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
plays a peculiar role in Assumptions B1–B14.

This section supplements the experimental basis for the light quantum hypothesis.
Einstein’s 1905 paper
[
7
]
was the first to point out that individual events in the photoelectric effect cannot be adequately represented by classical electromagnetism.
Einstein used this discovery as a starting point to construct the light quantum hypothesis based on energy conservation in individual trials and wave-particle duality
[
7
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
]
.
On the other hand, Bohr’s 1913 paper
[
45
]
was the first to suggest that individual events in the photoelectric effect could be explained without the light quantum hypothesis.
The traditional explanation of the light quantum hypothesis is that it was verified when the Bohr-Kramers-Slater (BKS) theory
[
49
]
proposed by Bohr was experimentally refuted
[
64
,
69
,
70
]
.
However, with the subsequent quantization of atomic systems and radiation fields by non-relativistic QED
[
38
,
40
]
, and the development of wave representations of radiation fields through the quantum theory of coherence
[
41
]
and semi-classical theory of radiation
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
, it is now possible to explain the statistical properties of radiation fields without using the light quantum hypothesis
[
37
,
47
]
.
To confirm this, the upper part of Table
3
shows literature on explaining the statistical properties of radiation fields.
As the purpose is to confirm known facts, citations are limited to Einstein’s paper and standard textbooks. While relativistic calculations
[
75
,
76
]
are necessary for computing the spectral shape of Compton scattering, the frequency-angle spectrum of inelastic scattering
[
74
]
can be semi-quantitatively explained based on phase matching conditions even within the scope of non-relativistic QED.

Therefore, in modern times, the only experimental facts that could serve as evidence for the existence of photons are the discontinuity of individual events and deterministic relations between individual events.
The lower part of Table
3
summarizes the status of explanations for the discontinuity of individual events and deterministic relations.
The problem with modern quantum optics theory is that, due to constraints of standard formulation (Section
1
), it cannot directly represent the discontinuous changes in the numbers of the emitted photoelectron or the deterministic relations in scattering directions in the Compton effect.
This is likely why the concept of photons could not be eliminated from quantum optics theory despite repeated criticisms.
This situation is aptly expressed by Glauber’s statement, “A photon is what a detector detects.”
However, as the light quantum hypothesis belongs to old quantum theory, its explanations of deterministic relations between spins and quantum jumps between stationary states are insufficient.
In contrast, our theory can explicitly represent discontinuous changes in the occurrence of individual events (Supplementary Material B) and various deterministic relations (§
5.4.1
, Supplementary Material B) by using logical variables to represent the occupation of stationary states.
Therefore, while our theory has constraints due to the non-relativistic approximation, it is considered to be a potential replacement for the light quantum hypothesis as a substructure of non-relativistic QED.

Supplementary material B: Examples

B.1
Photoelectric effect in hydrogen atom

{\bm{R}}={\bm{0}}

{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}

\{\!\!\{+\}\!\!\}
: Scattering states (
E(\phi^{\bm{r}})\,{>}\,0
)

\{\!\!\{-\}\!\!\}
: Bound states (
E(\phi^{\bm{r}})\,{\leqq}\,0
)

Figure 5:
Schematic of the photoionization of a hydrogen atom. A hydrogen atom, initially in one of the bound states, undergoes a discontinuous transition to one of the scattering states upon exposure to monochromatic light. The left figure shows a simplified energy diagram of the relative motion.
\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}
and
\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}
denote the entire sets of the bound and scattering states, where
-
and
+
stand for the sign of the eigenenergy
E(\phi^{\bm{r}})
. The right figure schematically shows the trajectories of the emitted electron and proton detected, for example, by a cloud chamber. The coordinates of the electron and proton are denoted by
{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}
and
{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}
, respectively. The emission directions are correlated even in a single trial, as shown in Compton scattering experiments
[

,

,
