]
.

B.1.1
General formula

In this section, we demonstrate the calculation of the photoelectric effect in a hydrogen atom
[
34
,
75
]
as an example where analytical calculation is possible.
First, based on our model of reality, we model the individual events of the photoelectric effect in a hydrogen atom as a transition from a bound state to a scattering state of the hydrogen atom (Assumptions B1-B8):

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`ionized,\,$t$'}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{The atom has been photoionized by $t$}\\
0,&\mbox{The atom has not been photoionized by $t$}\end{cases}

(335)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}}),0$'}\Big),\quad t\,{>}\,0,\quad j\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{N}}.

(336)

Here,
j
is the trial number, and
\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}
and
\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}
represent all bound states and scattering states of the atom, respectively (Fig.
5
).
The expectation value of this logical variable is defined for the initial ensemble specified by the proposition ‘
{\mathrm{A}}_{0},0
’ and density operator
\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})
as (Assumption B9):

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}\big)\big\rangle\equiv\frac{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'}}{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}\big)}{\sum_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'}}1}.

(337)

This has the meaning of the probability of the proposition ‘ionized,
t
’.
Therefore, to find the rate of occurrence of the photoelectric effect, it is sufficient to calculate the expectation value of the logical variable using the laws of quantum theory (Assumptions B10-B13) and Eq.(
336
), and then find its time derivative.
The specific calculation process is as follows:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,\Big]

(338)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}}),t$'${\wedge}$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}}),0$'}\Big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,\Big]

(339)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}}),0$'}\Big)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\Big)\,{\hat{z}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\Big)\,{\hat{z}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}}),0$'}\Big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,\Big]

(340)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}\Big[\,\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\,{\hat{z}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}})$'}\Big)\,\hat{U}(t)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,\Big]

(341)

Here,
\hat{U}(t)\,{\equiv}\,\exp({t}\hat{H}/{{\rm{i}}\hbar})
is the time evolution operator, and we know that the partial characteristic operator takes the following form according to the Born rule:

\displaystyle{\hat{z}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}})$'}\Big)=\sum_{|\phi\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}}|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|,\quad{\hat{z}}\Big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}})$'}\Big)=\sum_{|\phi\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}}|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|

(342)

To obtain Eq.(
341
), we assumed that the hydrogen atom is in a bound state at the initial time.
Also, due to the orthogonality of partial characteristic operators, the compatibility condition (Assumption B14) holds between propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}})
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}})
’, so the use of logical product (
\wedge
) in Eq.(
336
) and Eq.(
339
) is justified. Eq.(
341
) can be evaluated under the first-order perturbation approximation

\displaystyle\hat{U}(t)\cong\hat{U}_{0}(t)+\hat{U}_{1}(t)

(343)

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{0}(t)\equiv\exp\left(\frac{t}{{\mathrm{i}}\hbar}\hat{H}_{0}\right),\quad\hat{U}_{1}(t)\equiv\hat{U}_{0}(t)\,\frac{1}{{\rm{i}}\hbar}\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{I}}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}

(344)

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{I}}(t)\equiv\hat{U}^{\dagger}_{0}(t)\hat{H}_{\mathrm{I}}\hat{U}_{0}(t)

(345)

and the long-wavelength approximation, resulting in the following form
[
15
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}\big)\big\rangle\cong{\rm{Tr}}\left[\,\Big(\,{\tilde{\bm{A}}^{(-)}_{{\mathrm{T}}}}({\bm{R}},t)\,{\cdot}\,\hat{\bm{\kappa}}\,{\cdot}\,{\tilde{\bm{A}}^{(+)}_{{\mathrm{T}}}}({\bm{R}},t)\,\Big)\,\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,\right]\phantom{\Big|}

(346)

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\kappa}}\cong 2\pi\sum_{|\phi\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{+}\}\!\!\}}\sum_{|\phi^{\prime}\rangle}^{\{\!\!\{{-}\}\!\!\}}(\hbar\omega_{\phi}-\hbar\omega_{\phi^{\prime}})^{2}\langle{\phi^{\prime}}|\hat{\bm{D}}|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|\hat{\bm{D}}|\phi^{\prime}\rangle\,|\phi^{\prime}\rangle\langle{\phi^{\prime}}|

(347)

Here,
{\bm{R}}
(
{\in}\,{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
) is the position of the center of mass of the hydrogen atom,
\hat{\bm{D}}\equiv\sum_{k}^{{\mathrm{e}},{\mathrm{p}}}e_{k}\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}
is the electric dipole moment operator of the hydrogen atom, and
{\tilde{\bm{A}}^{(+)}_{{\mathrm{T}}}}
and
{\tilde{\bm{A}}^{(-)}_{{\mathrm{T}}}}
are the positive and negative oscillation parts of the transverse component of the vector potential in the interaction picture.
Eq.(
347
) matches the formula for light detection probability known in the field of quantum optics
[
16
, §14.2]
, confirming the validity of the modeling in Eq.(
336
).

B.1.2
Frequency-angle spectrum of the photoelectric effect in hydrogen atom

For more specific calculations, we need to separate the Hamiltonian of the hydrogen atom
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}
and the Hamiltonian of the radiation field
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{F}}
, determine the specific stationary states, and further specify the form of the density operator.
Here, we consider the case where, at the initial time, the relative motion of the hydrogen atom is in the 1s state
|\mathrm{1s}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}
, the spin is in the singlet state
|0\rangle^{\!\mathrm{S}}
, and the center of mass is in a quantum state
|{\bm{0}}\rangle^{\!\bf R}
near the origin at rest.
The quantum state
|{\bm{0}}\rangle^{\!\bf R}
is not an eigenstate of the hydrogen atom Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{A}}
, but a proxy representation (§
5.2.5
) of the stationary state of the entire system localized by interaction with the external environment.
Also, we assume that only the
{\bm{k}}\lambda
mode of the radiation field is in the stationary state
|\mbox{rad}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}
, and other modes are in the vacuum state
|\mbox{vac}\rangle^{\!\overline{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}
.
Here,
{\bm{k}}\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
is the wave vector, and
\lambda\,{\in}\,\{1,2\}
represents the polarization component.

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})

\displaystyle=|{\mathrm{1s}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}{}^{\bm{r}}\!\langle{\mathrm{1s}}|\otimes|0\rangle^{\!\mathrm{S}}{}^{\mathrm{S}}\!\langle 0|\otimes|{\bm{0}}\rangle^{\!\bf R}{}^{\bf R}\!\langle{\bm{0}}|\otimes|\mbox{rad}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}{}^{{\bm{k}}\lambda}\!\langle\mbox{rad}|\otimes|\mbox{vac}\rangle^{\!\overline{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}\,{}^{\!\overline{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}\!\langle\mbox{vac}|

(348)

In the following, to separate the motion of the atomic system and the radiation field, we consider cases where the radiation field
|\mbox{rad}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}
at the initial time is either the
n\,{=}\,1
Fock state
|\mbox{1}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}
or the coherent state
|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}
(
\tilde{\alpha}\,{\in}\,{\mathbb{C}}
).

Then, by substituting Eq.(
342
) and Eq.(
348
) into equation Eq.(
341
), under the first-order perturbation approximation and long-wavelength approximation, we obtain

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`ionized,$t$'}\big)\big\rangle=\iiint{}^{\bm{r}}\!\langle{\bm{p}}|\,\hat{\rho}^{\bm{r}}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,|{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}\,d{\bm{p}}.

(349)

Here, we denoted the scattering state with momentum
{\bm{p}}
as
|{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}
.
The scattering state
|{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}
includes a plane wave with wave number
{\bm{p}}/\hbar
and a near-field term decaying as
{\approx}\,1/\|{\bm{r}}\|

[
75
]
.
Also, the reduced density operator for relative motion
\hat{\rho}^{\bm{r}}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})
can be expressed under the above conditions as
[
15
]

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}^{\bm{r}}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},t_{0}$'})\cong\hat{U}^{\bm{r}}(t)|\mathrm{1s}\rangle\langle\mathrm{1s}|\hat{U}^{\bm{r}}(t)=|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}{}^{\bm{r}}\!\langle\psi(t)\big|.

(350)

Here, we defined the time evolution operator for relative motion
\hat{U}^{\bm{r}}(t)
as

\displaystyle\hat{U}^{\bm{r}}(t)\equiv\begin{cases}\displaystyle{}^{{\bm{k}}\lambda}\!\langle 1|\,\hat{U}^{{\bm{r}}{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}_{0}(t)\,|1\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}+{}^{{\bm{k}}\lambda}\!\langle{0}|\,\hat{U}^{{\bm{r}}{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}_{1}(t)\,|1\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}\phantom{\Big|}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{the case $|\mbox{rad}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}=|1\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}$}\\
\displaystyle{}^{{\bm{k}}\lambda}\!\langle\tilde{\alpha}|\,\hat{U}^{{\bm{r}}{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}_{0}(t)\,|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}+{}^{{\bm{k}}\lambda}\!\langle\tilde{\alpha}|\,\hat{U}^{{\bm{r}}{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}_{1}(t)\,|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}\phantom{\Big|}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{the case $|\mbox{rad}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}=|\tilde{\alpha}\rangle^{\!{\bm{k}}\lambda}$}.\end{cases}

(351)

with

\displaystyle\hat{U}^{{\bm{r}}{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}_{0}(t)\equiv{}^{\mathrm{S}}\!\langle 0|{}^{\bf R}\!\langle{\bm{0}}|\,{}^{\!\overline{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}\!\langle\mbox{vac}|\,\hat{U}_{0}(t)\,|\mbox{vac}\rangle^{\!\overline{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}|{\bm{0}}\rangle^{\!\bf R}|0\rangle^{\!\mathrm{S}}

(352)

\displaystyle\hat{U}^{{\bm{r}}{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}_{1}(t)\equiv{}^{\mathrm{S}}\!\langle 0|{}^{\bf R}\!\langle{\bm{0}}|\,{}^{\!\overline{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}\!\langle\mbox{vac}|\,\hat{U}_{1}(t)\,|\mbox{vac}\rangle^{\!\overline{{\bm{k}}\lambda}}|{\bm{0}}\rangle^{\!\bf R}|0\rangle^{\!\mathrm{S}}.

(353)

This time evolution operator for relative motion corresponds to the time evolution operator in the semiclassical theory of radiation, as the operators of the radiation field are replaced by matrix elements.
Therefore, the state vector of the relative motion of the hydrogen atom in this representation

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}\equiv\hat{U}^{\bm{r}}(t)|\mathrm{1s}\rangle,\quad t>0

(354)

becomes a pure state vector of the atomic system.

Particularly, when the frequency of the radiation field is sufficiently high such that
\nu\,{\gg}\,|E({\mathrm{1s}})|{/}h
, we can express the state vector of the hydrogen atom
|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}
as a superposition of the 1s state and scattering states:

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}=c_{\mathrm{1s}}(t)\,|\mathrm{1s}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}+\iiint c_{\bm{p}}(t)\,|{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}\,d{\bm{p}},\quad t>0

(355)

In this case, due to the unitarity of
\hat{U}^{\bm{r}}(t)
, the following relation holds:

\displaystyle\big|c_{\mathrm{1s}}(t)\big|^{2}+\iiint\big|c_{\bm{p}}(t)\big|^{2}\,d{\bm{p}}=1.

(356)

Also, by substituting Eq.(
350
) and Eq.(
355
) into Eq.(
349
) and differentiating with respect to time, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\iiint|c_{\bm{p}}(t)\big|^{2}\,d{\bm{p}}

(357)

\displaystyle=\iiint\lim_{\Delta{t}{\to}0}\frac{1}{\Delta{t}}\left(|c_{\bm{p}}(t+\Delta{t})\big|^{2}-|c_{\bm{p}}(t)\big|^{2}\right)\,d{\bm{p}}.

(358)

Furthermore, by rewriting Eq.(
357
) using Eq.(
356
), and evaluating
c_{\bm{p}}(t\,{+}\,\Delta{t})
in Eq.(
358
) using first-order perturbation approximation, we obtain the following equation after going through the equation transformations described in references
[
34
]
and
[
75
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{d}{dt}\big|c_{\mathrm{1s}}(t)\big|^{2}

(359)

\displaystyle\approx\mbox{const.}\iiint\frac{\cos^{2}({\bm{p}},{\bm{e}}_{\lambda})}{\nu^{7/2}}\,\delta\left(\frac{\|{\bm{p}}\|^{2}}{2m}+\Big|E_{\mathrm{1s}}\Big|-h\nu\right)d{\bm{p}}\,\mbox{(light power)}\big|c_{\mathrm{1s}}(t)\big|^{2}.

(360)

Here,
{\bm{e}}_{\lambda}
on the right-hand side is the polarization vector (direction of the electric field), and
({\bm{p}},{\bm{e}}_{\lambda})
represents the angle between
{\bm{p}}
and
{\bm{e}}_{\lambda}
.
By integrating Eq.(
360
) with respect to time and applying the laws of Boolean logic (Assumption B6), we find that the probability of the hydrogen atom not being ionized decays according to the following equation:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{$\lnot$`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}\big)\big\rangle=\exp\big(\,{-}\,\gamma_{\mathrm{1s}}{t}\,\big)

(361)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathrm{1s}}\approx\mbox{const.}\iiint\frac{\cos^{2}({\bm{p}},{\bm{e}}_{\lambda})}{\nu^{7/2}}\,\delta\left(\frac{\|{\bm{p}}\|^{2}}{2m}+\Big|E_{\mathrm{1s}}\Big|-h\nu\right)d{\bm{p}}\,{\times}\,\mbox{(light power)}.

(362)

This result correctly reproduces the main features of the photoelectric effect: the exponential decay of the probability of the proposition
\lnot\mbox{`$\mathrm{ionized},\,t$'}
and the proportionality of the ionization rate to
\nu^{-7/2}\cos^{2}({\bm{p}},{\bm{e}}_{\lambda})

[
75
]
.
Thus, we have rationally explained both the continuous (Eq.(
361
)) and discontinuous (Eq.(
336
)) aspects of the photoelectric effect in hydrogen atoms within the framework of non-relativistic QED, based on Assumptions B1-B14.

B.2
Classical trajectriess and EPR paradox

B.2.1
Phenomenological representation

Figure 6:
Schematic illustration of the measurement of the electron position. (a) Presence or absence of water droplets as represented by
x^{(j)}(\mbox{`$\mathrm{droplet}_{{\mathcal{R}}},t$'})
(black:0, white:1). (b) The position of the latest water droplet as represented by
x^{(j)}(\mbox{`o$(\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}}\}\!\!\}),t$'})
(black:0, white:1).

Next, we consider the representation of motion trajectories of electrons and protons produced in the photoelectric effect of hydrogen atoms.
Traditionally, the representation of motion trajectories of electrons and photons produced in the Compton effect has been problematic.
However, as this paper limits its consideration to non-relativistic theory, we will consider electrons and protons produced in the photoelectric effect instead of the Compton effect.
This setting is not realistic experimentally due to the effect of electrons and protons being deflected by interaction with polar molecules in the cloud chamber.
However, theoretically, there is a clear correspondence with the EPR paradox, as shown below.

First, by dividing real space into discrete cells and denoting the center coordinates of each cell as
{\mathcal{R}}
, we can represent whether there is a water droplet in the cell at position
{\mathcal{R}}
using the following logical variable (Fig.
6
(a)):

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{droplet},{{\mathcal{R}}},t$'}\big)=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{When a water droplet exists in cell ${\mathcal{R}}$ at time $t$},\\
0,&\mbox{Otherwise}.\end{cases}

(363)

Using this logical variable, we can express the latest water droplet position
{\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}(t)
in the following form:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}(t_{h})=\sum_{{\mathcal{R}}}{\mathcal{R}}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{h}$'}\big),\quad h\in{\mathbb{N}}

(364)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}}\}\!\!\}}),t_{h}$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{droplet},{{\mathcal{R}}},t_{h}$'}\big)-{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`$\mathrm{droplet},{{\mathcal{R}}},t_{h-1}$'}\big)

(365)

Here,
h
(
{\in}\,{\mathbb{N}}
) is the sequential number of the cell where the water droplet appeared, and
t_{h}
represents the time when a water droplet appeared in the
h
-th cell.

\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}}\}\!\!\}
is the set of stationary states of the entire system including charged particles and water droplets where a water droplet exists at
{\mathcal{R}}
.

\sum_{{\mathcal{R}}}
represents the sum over all cells.

B.2.2
Physical quantities in systems with and without measurement instruments

Furthermore, we consider cases where different charged particle trajectories can be distinguished based on the continuity of water droplet trajectories.
In this case, we can phenomenologically write the electron position
{\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{e}}(t)
and proton position
{\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{p}}(t)
in the following form:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{R}}_{k}}{\mathcal{R}}_{k}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{R}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big),\quad k\in\{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{p}\}

(366)

Here,
\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}_{k}}\}\!\!\}
is the set of stationary states of the entire system including water droplets where the water droplet created by the
k
-th charged particle is at
{\mathcal{R}_{k}}
.
Particularly, in measurements with an applied magnetic field, by substituting the curvature radius of the trajectory and the magnetic field into the classical mechanics formula, we can estimate the electron momentum
{\mathcal{P}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{e}}(t)
and proton momentum
{\mathcal{P}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{p}}(t)
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}{\mathcal{P}}_{k}\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'}\big),\quad k\in\{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{p}\}.

(367)

Here,
\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{P}_{k}}\}\!\!\}
is the set of stationary states of the entire system corresponding to the water droplet configuration that formed the basis for the estimated momentum value
{\mathcal{P}_{k}}
.
While it is difficult to explicitly write down the functional form of the stationary states constituting
\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}_{k}}\}\!\!\}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{P}_{k}}\}\!\!\}
, the stationary states constituting
\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}_{k}}\}\!\!\}
and
\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{P}_{k}}\}\!\!\}
are orthogonalized to each other by definition. Therefore, the compatibility condition holds between the propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{R}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ (
k\,{\in}\,\{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{p}\}
) and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{P}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}}),t
’ (
k^{\prime}\,{\in}\,\{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{p}\}
):

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{P}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{k^{\prime}}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{P}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{k^{\prime}}}\big)$'}\big)\,\hat{\wp}(\,t\,|\mbox{${\mathcal{A}}_{0},0$})\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{P}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{k^{\prime}}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{P}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{k^{\prime}}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{\mathcal{R}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}\big)$'}\big)\,\hat{\wp}(\,t\,|\mbox{${\mathcal{A}}_{0},0$})\,\Big],

\displaystyle\quad k\in\{\mathrm{e,p}\},\penalty\ k^{\prime}\in\{\mathrm{e,p}\}.

(368)

Here,
\hat{\wp}(\,t\,|\mbox{${\mathcal{A}}_{0},0$})
represents the density operator of the entire system including water droplets.
While Eq.(
368
) is a formal equation, it is consistent with the fact that position and momentum can be estimated with finite precision in experiments.

On the other hand, the objects of theoretical calculations are the positions and momenta of charged particles in an idealized system without water droplets.
The instantaneous values of these quantities can be defined by the following equations:

\displaystyle{\bm{r}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{r}}_{k}}{\bm{r}}_{k}\,{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}),t$'}),\quad k\in\{{\mathrm{e}},\,{\mathrm{p}}\}

(369)

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{p}}_{k}}{\bm{p}}_{k}\,{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}),t$'}),\quad k\in\{{\mathrm{e}},\,{\mathrm{p}}\}

(370)

Here,
\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}
is the set of eigenstates of the position operator
\hat{\bm{r}}_{k}
,
\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}
is the set of eigenstates of the momentum operator
\hat{\bm{p}}_{k}
, defined by

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{\,|\varphi\rangle\,\Big|\,\hat{{\bm{r}}}_{k}\,|\varphi\rangle\,{=}\,{\bm{r}}(\varphi)|\varphi\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\varphi|\varphi^{\prime}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}},\penalty\ {\bm{r}}(\varphi)\,{\in}\,\mbox{cell containing ${\bm{r}}$}\right\}

(371)

\displaystyle\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{\,|\varsigma\rangle\,\Big|\,\hat{{\bm{p}}}_{k}\,|\varsigma\rangle\,{=}\,{\bm{p}}(\varsigma)|\varsigma\rangle,\penalty\ \langle\varsigma|\varsigma^{\prime}\rangle\,{=}\,\delta_{\varsigma,\varsigma^{\prime}},\penalty\ {\bm{p}}(\varsigma)\,{\in}\,\mbox{cell containing ${\bm{p}}$}\right\}.

(372)

Also, the characteristic operators and partial characteristic operators of the propositions ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}),t
’ and ‘
{\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}),t
’ have the following form:

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{\!k}}\right)$'}\big)={\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{\!k}}\right)$'}\big)=\iiint_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell ${\bm{r}}$}}}\!\!\!|{\bm{r}}^{\prime}\rangle^{\!k}{}^{k}\!\langle{\bm{r}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bm{r}}^{\prime}

(373)

\displaystyle{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{\!k^{\prime}}}\right)$'}\big)={\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{\!k^{\prime}}}\right)$'}\big)=\iiint_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell ${\bm{p}}$}}}\!\!\!|{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\rangle^{\!k^{\prime}}{}^{k^{\prime}}\!\langle{\bm{p}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bm{p}}^{\prime}

(374)

B.2.3
Deterministic Relations

In experiments using cloud chambers, it’s common to identify the charged particle trajectories
{\bm{r}}^{(j)}_{k}
and
{\bm{p}}^{(j)}_{k}
(Eqs.(
369
)–(
370
)) with
{\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}_{k}
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{(j)}_{k}
(Eqs.(
366
)–(
367
)) determined by the water droplet configuration.
This relation can be expressed by the following deterministic relation:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}),t$'})={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{R}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{for}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\bm{r}}_{k}={\mathcal{R}}_{k},\penalty\ \penalty\ k\in\{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{p}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\forall}j

(375)

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}),t$'})={x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}}),t$'})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{for}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\bm{p}}_{k}={\mathcal{P}}_{k},\penalty\ \penalty\ k\in\{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{p}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\forall}j.

(376)

Also, by substituting Eqs.(
375
)–(
376
) into Eqs.(
366
)–(
367
) and Eqs.(
369
)–(
370
), we find the following relationships:

\displaystyle{\bm{r}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}_{k}(t),\penalty\ \penalty\ k\in\{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{p}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\forall}j

(377)

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}^{(j)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{P}}^{(j)}_{k}(t),\penalty\ \penalty\ k\in\{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{p}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\forall}j.

(378)

For the joint probabilities of physical quantities
{\bm{r}}_{k}
and
{\bm{p}}_{k}
in the idealized system without water droplets to match the joint probabilities of physical quantities
{\mathcal{R}}_{k}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{k}
in the realistic system with water droplets, the following compatibility condition must hold even in the subsystem without water droplets:

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{k^{\prime}}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{k^{\prime}}}\big)$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,\Big]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}^{\dagger}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{k^{\prime}}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k^{\prime}}}\}\!\!\}^{k^{\prime}}}\big)$'}\big)\,{\hat{z}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\big({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}}\big)$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,\Big],

\displaystyle\quad k\in\{\mathrm{e,p}\},\penalty\ k^{\prime}\in\{\mathrm{e,p}\},

(379)

This equation holds unconditionally due to the commutativity of partial characteristic operators when the charged particles are different (
k^{\prime}\,{\neq}\,k
).
However, when the charged particles are the same (
k^{\prime}\,{=}\,k
), because the position eigenstates and momentum eigenstates are not orthogonal, it’s necessary to lower the precision of the approximate representation of the subsystem’s physical quantities
{\bm{r}}_{k}
and
{\bm{p}}_{k}
to satisfy the compatibility condition.

B.2.4
Probabilistic interpretation of wave functions and deterministic relations

There are cases where the compatibility condition generally holds.
First, when the position uncertainty
\delta{\bm{r}}\,{\equiv}\,\delta{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}\,{=}\,\delta{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}
or momentum uncertainty
\delta{\bm{p}}\,{\equiv}\,\delta{\bm{p}}_{\mathrm{e}}\,{=}\,\delta{\bm{p}}_{\mathrm{p}}
is infinite.
When the momentum uncertainty
\delta{\bm{p}}
is infinite,
\hat{z}(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{p}}_{k}}\}\!\!\}^{k}})$'})\,{\cong}\,\hat{1}
, so the compatibility condition holds even when the position uncertainty
\delta{\bm{r}}
is arbitrarily small.
Particularly, in the problem of photoionization of hydrogen atoms, because the density operator (Eq.(
350
)) has the form

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\cong|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}{}^{\bm{r}}\!\langle\psi(t)|,\quad|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}=c_{\mathrm{1s}}(t)\,|\mathrm{1s}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}+\iiint c_{\bm{p}}(t)\,|{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}\,d{\bm{p}},

(380)

we can calculate the joint probability of electron and proton positions using the following equation:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}}\}\!\!\}^{\!{\mathrm{e}}}}),t$'$\wedge$`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}}\}\!\!\}^{\!{\mathrm{p}}}}),t$'}\big)\,\big\rangle

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\Big[\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}}\}\!\!\}^{\!{\mathrm{e}}}})$'}\big)\,{\hat{x}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}}\}\!\!\}^{\!{\mathrm{p}}}})$'}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,\Big]

(381)

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell ${\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}$}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!d{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}\iiint_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell ${\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}$}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!d{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}\left|\psi({\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}},{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}},t)\right|^{2},

(382)

where, we defined the two-particle wave function as

\displaystyle\psi({\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}},{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}},t)\equiv{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\langle{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}|{}^{\mathrm{e}}\!\langle{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}|{\bm{0}}\rangle^{\bf R}={}^{\bm{R}}\!\langle{\bm{R}}|{}^{\bm{r}}\!\langle{\bm{r}}|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}|{\bm{0}}\rangle^{\bf R}=\psi^{\bm{r}}({\bm{r}},t)\,\delta_{{\bm{R}},{\bm{0}}}.

(383)

This shows that the compatibility condition holds in the position representation (
\delta{\bm{p}}\,{\to}\,\infty
,
\delta{\bm{r}}\,{\to}\,0
), and the probabilistic interpretation of the two-particle wave function is valid.
However, in this case, because the momentum uncertainty is infinite, the joint probability of electron and proton momenta is meaningless.
Similarly, when the position uncertainty
\delta{\bm{r}}
is infinite, the compatibility condition holds even when the momentum uncertainty
\delta{\bm{p}}
is small, and the probabilistic interpretation of the wave function in the momentum representation is valid.

On the other hand, in the classical approximation (§
5.3
), if we take the representation precisions
\delta{\bm{r}}\,{=}\,(\delta{x},\delta{y},\delta{z})
and
\delta{\bm{p}}\,{=}\,(\delta{p}_{x},\delta{p}_{y},\delta{p}_{z})
to satisfy the complementarity principle:

\displaystyle\delta{x}\,\delta{p}_{x}\gg\frac{\hbar}{2},\quad\delta{y}\,\delta{p}_{y}\gg\frac{\hbar}{2},\quad\delta{z}\,\delta{p}_{z}\gg\frac{\hbar}{2},

(384)

the compatibility condition holds between any physical quantities defined as functions of position and momentum.
Particularly, in the problem of photoionization of hydrogen atoms, because the two-particle wave function is separable into relative motion and center-of-mass motion, we can write the state vector as

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!{\mathrm{e}},{\mathrm{p}}}

\displaystyle=|\psi(t)\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}|{\bm{0}}\rangle^{\bf R}=\sum_{{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}}\sum_{{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}}\psi({\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}},{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}},t)\big|{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}\big\rangle^{\!{\mathrm{e}}}\big|{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}\big\rangle^{\!{\mathrm{p}}}

(385)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{r}}\psi^{\bm{r}}({\bm{r}},t)\Big|\frac{m_{\mathrm{p}}}{m_{\mathrm{e}}+m_{\mathrm{p}}}{\bm{r}}\Big\rangle^{\!\!{\mathrm{e}}}\Big|-\frac{m_{\mathrm{e}}}{m_{\mathrm{e}}+m_{\mathrm{p}}}{\bm{r}}\Big\rangle^{\!\!{\mathrm{p}}},

(386)

showing that a entanglement occurs between the electron and proton positions.

Therefore, for the positions and momenta of electrons and protons produced by photoionization, due to the general properties of subsystems in quantum entanglement (§
5.1.3
), the following deterministic relations hold:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}}\}\!\!\}^{\mathrm{e}}}\right),t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`${\mathrm{o}}\left({\{\!\!\{{{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}}}\}\!\!\}^{\mathrm{p}}}\right),t$'}\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{for}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{e}}=-\frac{m_{\mathrm{p}}}{m_{\mathrm{e}}}{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{p}},\quad{\forall}j

(387)

Consequently, the following deterministic relation exists:

\displaystyle{\bm{r}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{e}}(t)=-\frac{m_{\mathrm{p}}}{m_{\mathrm{e}}}\,{\bm{r}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{p}}(t),\quad{\forall}j.

(388)

Similarly, for momentum, we can say that the following deterministic relation holds:

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{e}}(t)=-{\bm{p}}^{(j)}_{\mathrm{p}}(t),\quad{\forall}j

(389)

Thus, in our theory, the deterministic relations problematized in the EPR paradox hold.
However, because the instantaneous values of position and physical quantities are defined by Eqs.(
369
)–(
370
), and the precision of the propositions used in the definition is limited by the compatibility condition, positions and momenta do not have definite values in the sense of classical mechanics.
Additionally, in the Copenhagen interpretation, considering that position and momentum take definite values contradicts the premises of the interpretation itself.
In contrast, our theory is not based on the Copenhagen interpretation, so even if position and momentum take definite values, no logical contradiction arises.

B.3
Photomultiplier tube and Schrödinger’s cat

One of the main advantages of our theory is the ability to explicitly represent deterministic relations between successive individual events.
To see this, let us consider the case of amplifying an electron produced by the photoelectric effect using a photomultiplier tube.
In this case, we can phenomenologically represent the presence or absence of the photomultiplier tube’s output signal using the following logical variable:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`output,$t+\tau^{(j)}$'}\big)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\Big(\mbox{$\big(\lnot$`TN'$\wedge$`ionized,$t$'$\big)\,{\vee}\,$`FP'}\Big)

(390)

\displaystyle={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{$\lnot$`TN'}\big)\,{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`ionized',$t$'}\big)+{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`FP'}\big)

(391)

Here,
\tau^{(j)}
is the time delay between photoionization and the detection signal, and ‘TN’ and ‘FP’ are propositions of misdetection.
Physically, ‘TN’ represents a type of misdetection where no output signal is generated by the photomultiplier tube despite photoionization occurring (signal loss), and ‘FP’ represents a type of misdetection where an output signal is generated despite no photoionization occurring (amplification noise). The compatibility condition between propositions ‘ionized’, ‘TN’, and ‘FP’ holds if the characteristic operators of ‘TN’ and ‘FP’ have the form
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`TN'})\,{=}\,p_{\mathrm{TN}}\hat{1}
,
{\hat{x}}(\mbox{`FP'})\,{=}\,p_{\mathrm{FP}}\hat{1}
(§
3.4
).
Assuming that atomic photoionization and electron number amplification are independent phenomena (§
3.4
), and ignoring the variation in time delay
\tau^{(j)}
, we can evaluate the expectation value of Eq.(
390
) as follows:

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`output,$t+\tau$'}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\cong p_{\mathrm{TN}}\,\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`ionized,$t$'}\big)\big\rangle+p_{\mathrm{FP}}.

(392)

Eqs.(
390
)–(
392
) appropriately represent both the discontinuous and continuous aspects of the photodetector response.

Next, let us consider Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment triggered by the output pulse of a photomultiplier tube.
In our theory, we can phenomenologically represent the life or death of the cat using the following logical variable:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}(\mbox{`dead,$t$'})\equiv\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{The cat has been dead by time $t$},\\
0,&\mbox{Otherwise}.\end{cases}

(393)

Also, we can represent the assumption of the thought experiment (the cat dies if the atom is photoionized) by the following deterministic relation:

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`dead,$t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`output,$t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`ionized,$t$'}\big),\quad\forall{j}

(394)

For simplicity, we have omitted the propositions of erroneous determinations.
Here, if we define the proposition ‘alive’ as the negation of the proposition ‘dead’, we see that ‘dead’ and ‘alive’ are mutually exclusive (§
3.4
) by the laws of Boolean logic (Assumption B6):

\displaystyle{x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`dead,$t$'$\wedge$`alive,$t$'}\big)={x^{(j)}}\big(\mbox{`dead,$t$'$\wedge\lnot$`dead,$t$'}\big)=0,\quad\forall{j}

(395)

By taking the expectation value of Eq.(
394
) and using the calculation result of the photoionization rate (), we obtain

\displaystyle\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`dead,$t$'}\big)\big\rangle=\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`output,$t$'}\big)\big\rangle=\big\langle{x}\big(\mbox{`ionized,$t$'}\big)\big\rangle=1-\exp\big(\,{-}\,\gamma_{\mathrm{1s}}{t}\,\big).

(396)

This allows us to explain the exponential decay of the cat’s survival probability (
\langle{x}(\mbox{`alive,$t$'})\rangle

\,{=}\,1\,{-}\,\langle{x}(\mbox{`dead,$t$'})\rangle
) based only on the thought experiment’s assumption and Assumptions B1-B14.

Furthermore, it is natural to write the stationary states of the entire system including the cat and hydrogen atom as

\displaystyle|\mathrm{alive},\mathrm{1s}\rangle\equiv|\mathrm{alive}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}|\mathrm{1s}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}},\quad|\mathrm{dead},{\bm{p}}\rangle\equiv|\mathrm{dead}_{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}|{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}

(397)

and express the state vector of the entire system
|\psi(t)\rangle
as a superposition of stationary states:

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=c_{\mathrm{alive},\mathrm{1s}}(t)|\mathrm{alive},\mathrm{1s}\rangle+\iiint c_{\mathrm{dead},{\bm{p}}}(t)|\mathrm{dead},{\bm{p}}\rangle d{\bm{p}}

(398)

\displaystyle=c_{\mathrm{alive},\mathrm{1s}}(t)|\mathrm{alive}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}|\mathrm{1s}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}+\iiint c_{\mathrm{dead},{\bm{p}}}(t)|\mathrm{dead}_{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}|{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}d{\bm{p}}.

(399)

In this case, by defining the density operator of the entire system as

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})=\big|\psi(t)\big\rangle\big\langle\psi(t)\big|

(400)

and using the general properties of entangled subsystems, we see that the deterministic relation in equation (399) must exist.

In addition, in our theory, the density operator, evolving continuously according to the quantum equation of motion, changes discontinuously only when the ensemble is updated (§
3.3
).
When we condition the density operator on the life or death of the cat, we obtain equations equivalent to the projection postulate, namely

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}\,\big(\,t_{\mathrm{m}}\,|\,\mbox{`alive,\,$t_{\mathrm{m}}$'}\,\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{|{\mathrm{alive}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{alive}}|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,|{\mathrm{alive}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{alive}}|}{{\mathrm{Tr}}\big[\,|{\mathrm{alive}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{alive}}|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,|{\mathrm{alive}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{alive}}|\,\big]}

(401)

\displaystyle=|{\mathrm{alive}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{alive}}|\otimes|\mathrm{1s}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}{}^{\!\bm{r}}\!\langle\mathrm{1s}|

(402)

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}\,\big(\,t_{\mathrm{m}}\,\big|\,\mbox{`dead,\,$t_{\mathrm{m}}$'}\,\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{|{\mathrm{dead}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{dead}}|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,|{\mathrm{dead}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{dead}}|}{{\mathrm{Tr}}\big[\,|{\mathrm{dead}}\rangle\langle{\mathrm{dead}}|\,\hat{\rho}(\,t\,|\mbox{`${\mathrm{A}}_{0},0$'})\,|{\mathrm{dead}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{dead}}|\,\big]}

(403)

\displaystyle=|{\mathrm{dead}}\rangle^{\!\mathrm{cat}}{}^{\mathrm{cat}}\!\langle{\mathrm{dead}}|\otimes|\mathrm{sca}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}{}^{\!\bm{r}}\!\langle\mathrm{sca}|.

(404)

Here, we defined the state vector of the atom’s scattering state by

\displaystyle|\mathrm{sca}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}\equiv\iiint c_{\mathrm{dead},{\bm{p}}}(t)|{\bm{p}}\rangle^{\!\bm{r}}d{\bm{p}}.

(405)

Thus, we have explained the following properties of Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment:

•

Deterministic relationship between continuous individual events (Eq.(
394
))

•

Exclusive relationship between the cat’s life and death (‘dead,
t
’ and ‘alive,
t
’) in individual measurements (Eq.(
395
))

•

Exponential decay of the probability that the cat is alive (
{\mathrm{Pr}(\mbox{`alive,\,$t$'})}
) (Eq.(
396
))

•

Coherent superposition of state vectors (Eq.(
399
))

•

Discontinuous change of the density operator accompanying information update (Eqs.(
401
)–(
404
))

Therefore, in our theory, assumptions of non-causal collapse of the state vector, world splitting, or decoherence by the external environment are unnecessary to explain Schrödinger’s cat thought experiment.
